# About the Authors

![](images/Subhajit_Adhikari.jpg)Asst. Prof. Subhajit Adhikari is presently working at the RCC Institute of Information Technology, Kolkata. He had worked in BSH Department in Institute of Engineering & Management, Salt Lake, Kolkata. He had also worked in the BCA department in DAITM Kolkata. He has more than 8 years of teaching experience. Additionally, he is a Registered PhD student of the Department of Computer Science at the University of Burdwan, West Bengal. Moreover, he did his MSC in Computer Science from St. Xavier’s College, Kolkata and completed MTECH Software Engineering from MAKAUT(In-House), West Bengal. He was a gold medalist (First Class First) in MTECH Software Engineering in 2016\. He qualified for UGC-NET and WBSET in 2018 and 2019 respectively and has published several research papers in the domain of network security and cryptography. He received the “Young Scientist Award” in the field of Computer Science in 2023 from Dr. Ram Avatar Shiksha Samiti (DRASS) registered under the Societies Act 21, 1860 (Reg. No. 265, 2007-08) since July 2007\. He has been teaching “Java Programming” to undergraduate students for approximately 8 years. He also teaches “Artificial Intelligence” to the postgraduate students.

![](images/Sunil_Karforma.jpg)Prof. (Dr) Sunil Karforma has completed BE (Computer Science and Engineering) and ME (Computer Science and Engineering) from Jadavpur University. He has completed a Ph. D. in the field of Computer Science and he is presently holding the post of Dean (Science) between faculty and The University of Burdwan. Network security and e-commerce are his fields of research interest. He has published approximately 150 research papers in reputed National and International journals and proceedings. Also, he is the owner of 4 international patents and has successfully guided 12 PhD awardees.

# About the Reviewer

Jason Alls is an accomplished author of three programming books. He has been programming computers since 1999, and has worked on many different systems for UK, Australasian, and American companies. He has also worked on call centre management softwares, debt management systems, reporting systems, advertising software, warehouse software, and educational assessment software.

# Preface

Building real-life applications is a complex task that requires a deep understanding of modern technologies and programming languages. The most powerful and widely used tools in application development are C++ and Java.

This book is designed as a comprehensive guide for anyone interested in mastering the art of argument construction using Java. It takes you on a journey from the fundamentals of Java programming to more advanced topics, including object-oriented programming and the practical application of Java technology to create robust, scalable, and efficient software solutions.

In this book, you will explore the key features of Java and learn how to leverage them to create applications that are not only efficient and dependable but also easy to maintain and extend. The book emphasizes best practices in logic generation and application development, offering a wealth of practical examples, diagrams, and illustrations to facilitate your understanding of complex concepts.

Whether you are a newbie eager to learn the basics of Java programming, or an experienced developer aiming to expand your expertise and improve your application development skills, there is something to offer in this book. It highlights design patterns and advanced logic-building strategies essential for developing real-life applications.

By the time you complete this book, you will have acquired the knowledge and skills necessary to become a proficient developer in Java application development. I hope you will find this book both informative and useful as you begin or continue your journey into the world of programming.

[Chapter 1: Introduction to Java Programming](#ch01.xhtml) - This chapter talks about Java’s evolution and features and a comparative analysis of Java programming with C and C++. The details of a Java program syntax are outlined with the specifications required. This chapter also presents the characteristics essential for object-oriented programming with real-life examples. The relationship between Java Virtual Machine, Java Development Kit, and Java Runtime Environment is also discussed with diagrams with bytecode.

[Chapter 2: Fundamental Building Blocks in Java](#ch02.xhtml) - The concepts of data types of Javas, Java operators, Java expressions, conditional statements, and looping concepts are explained in this chapter. Let us first discuss the concept of a strongly typed language. Java is statically typed and a strongly typed language because, in Java, each type of data (such as integer, character, hexadecimal, packed decimal, and so forth) is predefined as part of the programming language.

[Chapter 3: Concept of Class, Objects, and Methods](#ch03.xhtml) - This chapter combines examples from everyday life and illustrations to explain the concepts of classes, objects, and methods. The model, blueprint, or design is the class. In this chapter, the characteristics of classes and class types are shown. Introducing the idea of an object, which is a class instance. This chapter also illustrates the causes of object generation. To help with comprehension, the differences between classes and objects are listed. Next, the notion of methods is presented. In OOPs, they are referred to as the functions inside the class. In this case, the method call procedure is shown. The unique type of method with the same name as the class is called constructors. Examples of code are used to illustrate the constructor’s idea. This chapter also covers this term and the crucial idea of method overloading.

[Chapter 4: Concepts of Array, String and Vectors in Java](#ch04.xhtml) - With sufficient knowledge of class, objects, methods, and constructors we are going to present some interesting and important topics, like array, string, vector, and wrapper class in this chapter. In different applications of mathematics, array is inseparable. String handling is also frequent in different real-life applications. String class methods like concat (), length() and substring() are very useful for manipulating different strings. In this chapter, we have presented a detailed explanation with coding examples of some of these methods. The StringBuffer class also explored with examples in this context. The class vector is also studied with diagrams for better understanding. The concept of auto-boxing and auto-unboxing with respect to wrapper class is included at the end of the chapter. After reading this chapter, students can learn from the basic to advanced level knowledge about the some of the most important programming constructs like array, String, vector, and wrapper class used for application development though java programming.

[Chapter 5: Types of Inheritance in Java](#ch05.xhtml) - In the previous chapter, the study of arrays, string and vectors has been discussed in detail. Now, we will focus on the concepts of inheritance and the need of inheritance along with the different types of inheritance. Then, we will cover the most important concept related to inheritance, its method overriding and how it is achieved with programs. Also, the usage of super keywords is added with different contexts. The functionality of the final variable, class and methods to restrict the subclass to inherit the properties and behaviors of the base class, will be studied in this chapter. The concept of interfaces will be presented to achieve multiple inheritance in java. Last, but not least, the different types of access modifiers are given with code and diagrams for better understanding of the learners.

[Chapter 6: Concept of Thread in Java](#ch06.xhtml) - In this chapter, the concept of multi-threading is placed in such a way that everyone can learn multi-threading to develop real life applications. The thread life cycle is also presented in a diagram with a brief description. Different ways of creating thread in java are included with proper examples. The detailed discussion of thread class and runnable interface is one of the most important parts of this chapter along with thread priority. Important methods like join(), and isAlive() associated with thread programming have been elaborated in this chapter. Last but not least, the concepts of thread synchronization and inter thread communication have been added to this chapter.

[Chapter 7: Exception Handling in Java](#ch07.xhtml) - Every day, everyone faces some unexpected events, in programming, this is known as an exception. When an exception has occurred, the most important thing is to describe the necessary information about the exception. Next, we have to overcome the exceptional event, in programming, it is known as exception handling. In this chapter, if a program code generates one exception, the way to handle it is described in detail. In this regard, the term error is defined first and then how it can lead to generate exception is presented. The different types of errors are discussed. Then, exception is defined and how exception handling can be done in java is elaborated using programs.

[Chapter 8: Package Creation in Java](#ch08.xhtml) -  Packages are an essential notion in Java. Java uses packages to eliminate name conflicts and manage access to classes, interfaces, and enumerations. The Java platform has an extensive class library (a collection of packages) that may be used in your own applications. This library is called the Application Programming Interface (API). Its packages cover the most typical tasks connected with programming for general use. A String object, for example, consists of state and behavior for character strings; a File object enables a programmer to easily create, delete, inspect, compare, or modify a file on the file system; a Socket object enables the creation and use of network sockets; and various GUI objects control buttons, check boxes, and other aspects of graphical user interfaces. There are literally hundreds of courses to pick from. This enables you, the programmer, to concentrate on the design of your specific application rather than the infrastructure needed to make it work.

[Chapter 9: Stream and File Handling in Java](#ch09.xhtml) -  In Java, a stream is a series of data that is ordered. Java offers streams as a broad method of handling data I/O. To enable input and output operations in Java, the Java I/O system links a stream to a physical layer. In this chapter, we are going to present the detailed concept of stream and its types with pictorial representation. Then different classes and methods of different stream classes will be discussed. Also, the file handling in java is presented in this chapter. The relationship between streams and files are elaborated in this context.

[Chapter 10: Applet Program in Java](#ch10.xhtml) - Applet is a small program in java. It is not a standalone program. To implement the applet program, we need browser support. It has its own life cycle. There are several methods present inside applet class to implement applet lifecycle. In this chapter, we are elaborating the building blocks of the applet programming using java. To give clear pictures of each concept, we have taken simple programs. The html programming is also taken into consideration to run applet programs. There is several programming examples are written in this chapter to demonstrate the utility and usability of applet programming building real life applications.

[Chapter 11: Miscellaneous Programs Using Java](#ch11.xhtml)- In this chapter, first we have provided the source code of two of the most important data structures Stack and Queue. Then, the Tic-tac-toe game is demonstrated using java program. Later, the two important symmetric key and asymmetric key algorithm AES and RSA have been implemented using java programming for encryption and decryption. At the end, the socket programming has been added in form of message passing between two programs, chat application and file sending between client and server.

# [CHAPTER 1Introduction to Java Programming](#contents.xhtml_ch01a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_1a)

This chapter talks about Javaʼs evolution and features and a comparative analysis of Java programming with C and C++. The details of a Java program syntax are outlined with the specifications required. This chapter also presents the characteristics essential for object-oriented programming with real-life examples. The relationship between Java Virtual Machine (JVM), Java Development Kit (JDK), and Java Runtime Environment (JRE) is also discussed with diagrams with byte code.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_2a)

The chapter covers the following topics:

*   Introducing Java
*   History of Java
*   Features of Java
*   Relationship between JVM, JRE, and JDK
*   Bytecode

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_3a)

This chapter aims to cover the history of Java programming, tracing its origins through a programming language family tree. It also explores the shift from procedural to object-oriented programming, highlighting reasons for the transition. The chapter explains the differences between C, C++, and Java programming constructs, emphasizing Javaʼs features in an object-oriented context with examples. Additionally, it explores the architecture of the Java Virtual Machine, focusing on byte code operations. The goal is to provide readers with a comprehensive understanding of Javaʼs evolution, principles, and runtime environment.

[Introducing Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_4a)

Everyone uses language as a mode of communication to spread ideas and arguments to others regularly. A programming language created artificially directs how a machine, especially a computer, behaves. Syntactic (the syntax of a program code) and semantic (meaning of a program code) principles are used to design programming languages, just as human languages are used to specify their structure and meaning, respectively. Programming languages are used to define algorithms accurately and ease communication about organizing and manipulating information. The goal of a programming language is to make it possible for people to provide instructions to computers, allowing the machines to behave in a predetermined and desired way. The family tree of programming languages is shown in [Figure 1.1](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-1):

![](images/Figure_1.1.png)

Figure 1.1: Family tree of important programming languages

Now consider the most trending languages in 2022\. This is shown as per the job requirement in [Figure 1.2](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-2). Java is the second most used programming language according to web application development.

![](images/Figure_1.2.png)

Figure 1.2: Top programming languages 2022

Java is a general-purpose language, often referred to as a strongly typed language. It is commonly used for object-oriented programming. Java program codes are typically compiled to the bytecode instruction set and binary representation defined in the JVM specification. The program codes written in Java can be categorized as Applications, Apps, Applets, or Servlets.

Java programs, which run locally and independently on a computer, are called applications. Applications and apps are similar, although the term app is usually used to describe software that runs on mobile devices, such as mobile phones. Applets are Java programs downloaded over the internet and run by a JVM, which is normally included with the browser.

The Servlet programs are Java programs created for a web server to run and give functionality to websites. Even in modern web development, this type of Java program is frequently used. In the next section, we will briefly discuss the history of Java.

[History of Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_5a)

Sun Microsystems created Java in the early-to-mid 1990s, and the development continues to this day. A team headed by James Gosling set up to work on a programming language for consumer electronic devices, and the language came to be known as Oak, which was then renamed to Java. Here are some of the more significant years in the Java languageʼs early history in [Figure 1.3](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-3):

![](images/Figure_1.3.png)

Figure 1.3: History of Java

[Transition from procedural programming to OOP](#contents.xhtml_sc3_6a)

There are several reasons for adopting Object-Oriented Programming (OOP) rather than procedural programming. Some of them are as follows:

*   The data of a system and the operations that alter the data are often kept separate in procedural programming. The main benefit of object-oriented programming is that the data and the operations that alter the data or the code are both contained within the object. As opposed to procedural or structured design, where attributes and behaviors are often separated, object-oriented design integrates characteristics and behaviors into a single object.
*   Comparing an object-oriented model to procedural programming, we can say that in terms of proper design, there is no such thing as global data. In object-oriented systems, this characteristic guarantees a high level of data integrity. The terms attributes and methods are used in object-oriented programming to describe data and behaviors. Data hiding refers to the practice of restricting access to specific properties and/or methods.

Let us examine how procedural and object-oriented programming languages vary from one another, as shown in [Table 1.1](#ch01.xhtml_tab1-1):

| Procedural | OOP |
| Programming is done based on task or procedure | Object is used rather than tasks |
| A task is known as a small part of a problem. | Object represents a person, a table or a clock. It has functions and characteristics |
| It follows Top-down approach. | It follows Bottom-Up approach. |
| It does not support access modifiers. | It supports access modifiers |
| It is not easy to add new data and functions. | It is simple to add new data and functions. |
| It is less secure since it lacks a suitable method of hiding data. | It has the concept of data hiding so it is more secure. |
| The importance of function is more than data. | The importance of data is more important than function. |
| The concept of inheritance is not supported. | The concept of inheritance is supported in three modes: public, private & protected. |
| Example: C, , Fortran, VB, and Pascal. | Example- Simula, Smalltalk, C++ and Java |

Table 1.1: Comparison between procedural and OOP approach

[Comparison between C, C++ and Java programming](#contents.xhtml_sc3_7a)

There are several differences between C, C++, and Java language, some of which are given in [Table 1.2](#ch01.xhtml_tab1-2):

| C | C++ | Java |
| --- | --- | --- |
| C can only be compiled, not interpreted | C ++can only be compiled, not interpreted | Java language can be compiled and interpreted. |
| C supports both Pass by value and Pass by reference. | C++ supports both Pass by value and Pass by reference | Java supports only Pass by value technique |
| C does not support both operator and method overloading | C++ supports both operator and method overloading | Java supports only method overloading |
| C supports goto statement | C++ supports goto statement | Java does not support the goto statement |
| There is no concept of Inheritance in C. | In C++, both single inheritance and multiple inheritances are supported | Java supports the single inheritance, multilevel inheritance and hierarchical heritance using the extend keyword. |
| C supports Structure and Union | C++ supports Structure and Union | Java does not support Structure, and Union |
| In C, the runtime error detection is handled by the programmer | In C++, the runtime error detection is handled by the programmer | In Java, the runtime error detection is handled by the system |
| C uses #include for attaching header files to a program | C++ uses #include for attaching header files to a program | Java uses import keyword for attaching library classes to a program |
| The concept of namespace is not in C. | C++ uses the concept of namespace. There is no concept of package in C++ | Java uses the concept of package. There is no concept of namespace. |

Table 1.2: Comparison between C, C++ and Java language

[Features of Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_8a)

The main goal in designing Java is to make the program portable, simple and secure in nature. Among different important features of the Java programming language, some of them are listed as follows.

[Simple to use](#contents.xhtml_sc3_9a)

Java is easy to understand and learn since the syntax is quite simple. Now, in [Figure 1.4](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-4), we have discussed each term associated with a simple Java program to print the message Hello using the class test. The keywords public and static can be written as static public before main() method in a Java program, which means order is not mandatory to be same. For example: static public void main(String args[]) is a valid declaration.

![](images/Figure_1.4.png)

Figure 1.4: Syntax of a simple Java program

We can declare a string array to any acceptable form as given in the code:

class test

{

public static void main(String[] args)

{

System.out.println(“Hello”);

}

}

[Compiled and interpreted](#contents.xhtml_sc3_10a)

The process of compiling a Java program converts the comprehensible text coded by the programmer as program file (known as source code) to the bytecode representations, which are architecture neutral (known as platform-independent) instructions for the Java Virtual Machine (JVM).

Once your program has successfully been converted into Java bytecodes, you can interpret and run Java programs on any JVM or Java applets in any Web browser. Java is a two-stage system, as depicted in [Figure 1.5](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-5).

Stage 1: Java compiler translates the source code into bytecode instruction.

Stage 2: Java interpreter generates machine code that can be directly executed by the machine.

![](images/Figure_1.5.png)

Figure 1.5: Compilation and interpretation of Java program

[Object oriented](#contents.xhtml_sc3_11a)

The term object refers to a physical thing or a runtime thing, such as a pen, chair, table, computer, watch, and more. An approach or paradigm for creating programs that use classes and objects is called object-oriented programming. By presenting some notions, it makes program creation and maintenance simpler. Java is an object-oriented language, practically everything in Java is an object. Therefore, we are able to combine many sorts of objects with data and actions to create our program.

[Class and object](#contents.xhtml_sc3_12a)

A class is a model or blueprint or prototype from which objects are created. This section defines a class that models the state and behavior of a real-world object. It intentionally focuses on the basics, showing how even a simple class can cleanly model state and behavior. The syntax of class and objects is as follows:

class A

{

void disp()

{

System.out.println("Object of class A created");

}

}

class test

{

public static void main(String args[])

{

A ObjA=new A();

ObjA.disp();

}

}

In the preceding code, A is a class and ObjA is an object of A class. The output of the preceding code is given in [Figure 1.6](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-6):

![](images/Figure_1.6.png)

Figure 1.6: Creation of class and object

[Data abstraction](#contents.xhtml_sc3_13a)

The technique of concealing certain details and just displaying the userʼs necessary information is known as data abstraction. In other words, rather than learning how an object works, the user will be provided with knowledge on what it really performs. The concept of abstraction property can be achieved with either abstract classes or interfaces. The coding examples of how data abstraction can be achieved are given in [Figure 1.7](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-7):

![](images/Figure_1.7.png)

Figure 1.7: Data abstraction with the help of abstract class and interface

In the preceding code, the class A is acting as an abstract class, containing one abstract method which does not have the body and is defined in the subclass B. Abstract classes specify a protocol and guidelines that their subclasses must follow like subclass B.

In the preceding code, the class C implements the interface I, containing a method f() which has to be implemented in the class which implements class C.

[Polymorphism](#contents.xhtml_sc3_14a)

The ability to create in several forms is referred to as polymorphism. Let us say a man behaves differently depending on the circumstance. He acts differently when he is at home, in the office, or at a party. Humans are the same, yet they behave differently in various contexts. Polymorphism can be of two types. One is compile time polymorphism and other is runtime polymorphism. The concept of method overloading is implemented to show the compile time polymorphism and overriding is implemented to show the run time polymorphism. The coding examples are given in the [Figure 1.8](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-8):

![](images/Figure_1.8.png)

Figure 1.8: Implementation of Polymorphism using method overloading and method overriding

In the preceding example, the method testAdd() is overloaded to add the values of two numbers and three numbers respectively. In the other example, the method disp() is overridden in two different classes.

[Inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc3_15a)

An object can acquire or inherit the properties of another object through inheritance, which also permits hierarchical classification. The concept behind this is that we may develop new classes that are built on top of existing classes; in other words, when you inherit from an existing class, you can reuse its fields and methods. The parent-child connection is represented via inheritance. The following code demonstrates how the inheritance concept is implemented in Java programming:

class Base

{

int i=10;

}

class Derived extends Base

{

System.out.println(“the value of i is”+i);

}

class test5

{

public static void main(String args[])

{

Derived objD=new Derive();

objD.disp();

}

}

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_1.9.png)

Figure 1.9: Example of single Inheritance using Base class and Derived class

In the preceding coding example, using the concept of inheritance is implemented using two classes Base class and Derived class. The value of the variable is i (that is a data member of parent class), is initialized to 10 in the Base class and is used in Derived class. Thus the property (data member) of the base class is inherited in the derived class. Then the object of Derived class ObjD is created and is used to call the method disp() to print the value of the variable i (which is a data member of base class).

[Platform independence](#contents.xhtml_sc3_16a)

Javaʼs platform independence is one of its most significant characteristics. Applications created for one operating system can function on any other. When a Java application is compiled, a class File is created containing bytecode. JVM reads the bytes from the code. The byte code was produced on the Windows platform however it may be used in the Linux environment.

Considering the crucial point about Java, it is an architecture-neutral language (known as platform-independent language), but the JVM is architecture-dependent. So, all JVMs are designed for different OS, and the byte code (that is architecture neutral) is the same and is able to run on different OS. It is the reason why Java is platform-independent. The concept is shown in [Figure 1.10](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-10):

![](images/Figure_1.10.png)

Figure 1.10: Interpretation “.class” files in different platforms

[Robust and secure](#contents.xhtml_sc3_17a)

Java is a robust language. The robustness of a Java program depends upon three factors. Firstly, Java has a strict compilation and run-time checking for all data types. Secondly, Java has a good exception handling mechanism. Thirdly, memory management is very strong in Java. Java has an automatic garbage collection system to reclaim unused memory. The security feature demonstrates that Java provides a wide range of protection from viruses and malicious programs because JVM has strong security checking when a program executes. It ensures that there will be no damage and no security will be broken.

[Multi-threaded](#contents.xhtml_sc3_18a)

The multithreading programming feature in Java allows you to write a program that performs several different tasks simultaneously. The idea of multithreading is given in the code segment as follows:

import java.lang.Thread;

class Thrd1 extends Thread

{

public void run() {

for(int i = 1; i <=5; i++) {

System.out.println("Thread1" + " "+i);

}

}

}

public class test6

{

public static void main(String args[])

{

Thrd1 th1 = new Thrd1();

th1.start();

}

}

The preceding code segment is only demonstrating how the thread is created for the printing of values of a variable under Thread1. The output is given in [Figure 1.11](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-11):

![](images/Figure_1.11.png)

Figure 1.11: Thread 1 created from the main thread and it is running

[Distributed](#contents.xhtml_sc3_19a)

Distributed programming allows programmers to decrease the execution speed of a program using several computers spread over the Internet that are interlinked with each other. Java is designed for distributed Internet environments as it manages the TCP/IP protocol.

[Java as Internet language](#contents.xhtml_sc3_20a)

Java is sometimes referred to as the Internet language since the first Java-based application software was the Web browser HotJava, which allowed users to execute Internet applets. Applets may be created locally using HotJava and the Java programming language by Internet users.

[Java as communication tool](#contents.xhtml_sc3_21a)

The different classes used for networking are defined in java.net. The java programs can use protocols like TCP or UDP to communicate over the internet. Classes like Socket, URL, URLConnection, and ServerSocket use TCP to communicate over the network. The classes MulticastSocket, DatagramPacket and DatagramSocket are for use with UDP. The Java Communications 3.0 API is a Java extension that facilitates developing platform-independent communications applications for technologies such as Smart Cards, embedded systems, and point-of-sale devices, financial services devices, fax, modems, display terminals, and robotic equipment.

[Java in Human Machine Interface](#contents.xhtml_sc3_22a)

The hardware or software that allows an operator to communicate with a controller is known as the Human-Machine Interface (HMI). An HMI may be anything from a physical control panel with buttons and indication lights to an industrial PC running special HMI software that has a color graphics display.

As an application of HMI, Web panels in automation panel are becoming increasingly important in the context of Industry 4.0\. In Cannon’s The L1/C1 automation panels, the Java and HTML5 supported web browser is used for optimized communication.

[Relationship between JVM, JRE, and JDK](#contents.xhtml_sc2_23a)

To understand the relationship between JVM, JRE, and JDK, we have to understand JVM and its architecture. The Java Virtual Machine is an abstract computing machine. Like a real computing machine, it has an instruction set and manipulates various memory areas at run time.

[Java Virtual Machine](#contents.xhtml_sc3_24a)

The JVM is known as an interpreter for the interpretation of byte code produced. The JVM provides a runtime environment in which byte code can be executed. The JVM is platform dependent. The JVM, which is the abstract machine, does not exist physically, but the JRE does. The JVM performs the following tasks:

*   Loads the code [loads the class file]
*   Verifies the code [verify the class file]
*   Executes the code [executes the actual code]
*   Provides run time environment

Let us discuss the detailed explanation of the above list of tasks in the next paragraph.

Suppose in the source code, the main() method has methods f1()and f2(). The main method is stored in file Test1.java, Func1() is stored in a file as Test2.java, Func2() is stored in a file as Test3.java, as shown in [Figure 1.12](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-12):

![](images/Figure_1.12.png)

Figure 1.12: Representation of “.class” files

We know that JVM is located in the RAM. Now, at the time of execution, using the class loader, the class files are brought on the RAM as given in [Figure 1.13](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-13):

![](images/Figure_1.13.png)

Figure 1.13: Representation of “.class” file before loading into in RAM

The verification of bytecode ensures that there are no security breaches present. The bytecode verifier acts as a sort of gatekeeper: it ensures that code passed to the Java interpreter is in a fit state to be executed and can run without fear of breaking the Java interpreter. The architecture of JVM and how it works is given in the [Figure 1.14](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-14):

![](images/Figure_1.14.png)

Figure 1.14: Working of JVM with its architecture

The code which is imported is not allowed to execute by any means until after it has passed the verifierʼs tests. Once all the work is done by the verifier, a number of important properties are found, which are as discussed below:

*   There will be no operand stack overflows or underflows.
*   In all the bytecode instructions, all the types of parameters are known to be always correct.
*   All the object field accesses are declared as private, public, or protected, known as legal.

JDK is the combination of JRE and development Tools. JRE consists of JVM and Library classes. JRE is the actual implementation of JVM. The relationship between JVM, JRE and JDK is given in the [Figure 1.15](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-15):

![](images/Figure_1.15.png)

Figure 1.15: Relationship between JVM, JRE and JDK

[Bytecode](#contents.xhtml_sc2_25a)

Bytecode is a platform-independent binary representation of source code. In Java, the format of compiled Java is known as Java bytecode. Java bytecode consists of operations that are one byte in length, together with their operands of variable size.

In [Table 1.3](#ch01.xhtml_tab1-3), we can find the bytecode instructions of a Java source code. Let us, explain the bytecode line by line. The iadd instruction pops the top two slots of the expression stack and pushes their sum. The iload instruction loads an int value from a local variable #index. The iconst 1 instruction loads the int value 1 onto the stack. The iadd instruction add two integers. The istore instruction stores int value into variable #index.

| Java source code | Byte code |
| intfunc(){intx,y,z;z=x+y+1;} | intfunc();iload xiload yiconst 1iaddiaddistore z |

Table 1.3: Bytecode generated from source code

An internal stack of operands is there in every program. There is some bytecode instruction that operates on that operand stack for the currently executing method. After pushing those values onto the stack, they are processed. Once on the stack, those values can act as inputs to an operation.

Consider an example, suppose we want to add the numbers 1 and 2\. Both the values must first get pushed onto the operand stack, by executing the bytecode instructions iconst_1 and iconst_2. With these two numbers on the stack, the iadd instruction can now consume both values by popping them off the top of the stack. As a result, the instruction pushes the sum of the consumed values back onto the stack. The step by step detailed operations are given in the following [Figure 1.16](#ch01.xhtml_fig1-16):

![](images/Figure_1.16.png)

Figure 1.16: How byte code is executed using operand stack

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_26a)

In this chapter, taking language as a communication tool, the concept of programming language is defined. The history of Java programming is also given. The transition from a procedural paradigm to object-oriented paradigm is discussed. All the important features of Java programming are studied with coding examples. The connections between JVM, JRE and JDK are talked about and explained.

In the next chapter, we will discuss the concepts of Data types, Operators, Conditional Statements and Loops in detail.

# [CHAPTER 2Fundamental Building Blocks in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch02a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_31a)

The concepts of data types of Java, Java operators, Java expressions, conditional statements, and looping concepts are explained in this chapter. Let us first discuss the concept of a strongly typed language. Java is statically typed and also a strongly typed language because, in Java, each type of data (such as integer, character, hexadecimal, packed decimal, and so forth) is predefined as part of the programming language.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_32a)

The chapter explains the following concepts:

*   Java as a strongly typed language
*   Tokens in Java
*   Hierarchy of data type
*   Non-primitive data Types or reference types
*   Operators in Java
*   Conditional statements
*   Loops in Java

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_33a)

The chapter aims to provide the basic details of tokens, data types, and variables with the concept of strongly typed language. The hierarchy of data types and types of variables are the important topics studied and widely examined in this chapter. The concepts of primitive and non-primitive data types have also been discussed with examples. This chapter explores the concept of typecasting in Java. Next, the world of Java operators has been studied in detail with examples with precedence. The important concepts like conditional statements and loops are elaborated with flowcharts in this chapter to express the technical aspect of Java programming that can lead to real-world application developments.

[Java as a strongly typed language](#contents.xhtml_sc2_34a)

In Java, thanks to its syntactic properties, the determination of the type of every variable and statement without running the program is possible. Also, all assignments and parameters that are explicit in nature when passing through method calls are eligible for type compatibility checking.

A Boolean value cannot be used as an integer, and double operations cannot be performed on an int value. The int value must first be converted to double format in order to interpret a value as something it is not. It is, therefore, impossible from a syntactic standpoint to have an operation disregard the types of its operands. Every variable has a type, every expression has a type, and every type is strictly defined. All assignments are checked for type compatibility. There is no automatic conversion of conflicting types. Any type of mismatches are errors.

All variables must be declared. You cannot assign to a variable before it is declared. The declaration specifies the type of the variable.

<type><variable>;

You can define a variable as a scalar:

int x;

float f;

After a variable is declared, you can assign a value to it.

int x;

x = 4;

Now, before delving into the concepts of different data types, we need to know about the different types of tokens in Java programming language.

[Tokens in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_35a)

The smallest programming constructs are known as tokens. There are five types of tokens in Java programming languages - keywords, literals, identifiers, separators, and operators. The details are discussed in the upcoming sections.

[Keywords in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_36a)

Keywords have their specific meaning defined in the compiler.

[Table 2.1](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-1) shows keywords for Java. Any of the following cannot be used as identities in your programs. Keywords like const and goto are considered reserved despite the fact that they are not currently in use. Terms like true, false, and null may appear as keywords, but they are literals; you cannot use them as identifiers in your applications.

|  | continue | for | new | switch |
| assert### | default | goto# | package | synchronized |
| boolean | do | if | private | this |
| break | double | implements | protected | throw |
| byte | else | import | public | throws |
| case | enum#### | instance of | return | transient |
| catch | extends | int | short | try |
| char | final | interface | static | void |
| class | finally | long | strictfp## | volatile |
| const# | float | native | super | while |

Table 2.1: Keywords in Java

As per the details given in the preceding table, we have used the # symbol to the power of the keywords to designate the usage and addition to the new releases of Java (JDK releases). The single # means it is not used. The double ## means the keyword is added to the version 1.2\. The triple ### means the keyword is added to the version 1.4\. The four #### means the keyword is added to the version 5.0.

[Literals in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_37a)

Each consistent assessment that can be authorized with the help of a variable is known as Literals. For example, int age=23;

In the example, age is the integer literal. We can write string literals as:

String m="Hello";

[Identifiers in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_38a)

Identifiers are the names of variables, classes, methods, packages, and interfaces. Identifiers are the words a programmer uses in a program. Most identifiers have no predefined meaning except as specified by the programmer. The rules for creating identifiers are given in the [Table 2.2](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-2):

| Rule no. | Rules |
| 1 | An identifier can consist of letters, digits, the underscore character (_), and the dollar sign ($) |
| 2 | The character appearing first must be any non-digit from the Unicode standard. |
| 3 | As per this rule, the subsequent characters may be taken as digits. |
| 4 | The character underscore "_" and $ for the first character are not permitted. |
| 5 | By nature, Java language is case-sensitive. So, Sum and sum are considered different identifiers. |

Table 2.2: Rules for identifiers in Java

[Separators in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_39a)

Separators are known as punctuators. There are some basic separators, which are listed in a tabular format in [Table 2.3](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-3) with their explanations:

| Symbol | Meaning |
| () | Specify a list of parameters, expressions, and precedence in the expression and it is used in type casing. |
| [] | Indicates index values |
| {} | Used for class definition, indicates code blocks, string, and array object initialization. |
| ; | Used as terminating symbol after each statement |
| , | Used for parameter list separation, variables separation, separation of expressions in for loop |
| . | Used for method calling using objects, data member initialization using objects, and maintaining the package hierarchy. |

Table 2.3: List of separators in Java

[Operators in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_40a)

An operator is a symbol that tells the computer to perform certain mathematical or logical manipulations. Operators are used in programs to manipulate data and variables. Java has arithmetic operators (+,-,*,/), assignment operators (=), comparison operators (<=,>=,==), logical operators (&&,||) and bitwise operators (&,|). They will be discussed later in this chapter. With the knowledge of the tokens, we are going to present the hierarchy of data types in Java in the next section.

[Hierarchy of data type](#contents.xhtml_sc2_41a)

Data types specify the different sizes and values that can be stored in the variable. There are two types of data types in Java ([Figure 2.1](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-1)):

*   Primitive data types: The primitive data types include Boolean, char, byte, short, int, long, float, and double. The predefined data types in Java by the language and named by the keywords are called primitive data types in Java.
*   Non-primitive data types: The non-primitive data types include classes, interfaces, and arrays.

![](images/Figure_2.1.png)

Figure 2.1: Hierarchy of data types

[Primitive data types](#contents.xhtml_sc3_42a)

There are several types of primitive data types. They are given in the [Figure 2.2](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-2) with their sizes:

![](images/Figure_2.2.png)

Figure 2.2: Examples of primitive data types

Let us discuss the different primitive data types in detail:

*   Boolean data type: The only two possible values for the Boolean data type are true and false, and it is used to represent logical values. Simple true/false flags are managed using this data type. Although only one piece of information may be specified by the Boolean data type, its exact size cannot be determined.

Example: Boolean one = false

*   Byte data type: Byte is the most basic integer type. As an illustration of a primitive data type, consider the byte, an integer with twoʼs complement and an 8-bit signed size. The range of its values is from (-2^(8-1)) 128 to (-2^(8-1) - 1) 127\. (inclusive). The value range for this variable is -128 to 127. By default, it has the value of 0\. In big arrays, when memory savings are most needed, the byte data type is applied to save memory. A byte takes up four times less storage than an integer. Hence, it saves space. Additionally, you may use it instead of the int data type.

Example: byte bt=127;

*   char data type: A single 16-bit Unicode character is represented by the char data type. Its value range is between u0000 (or 0) to \uffff (or 65,535 inclusive). Characters are stored in the char data type.

Example: char letter A = 'A'

*   short data type: A signed, 16-bit integer with two complements form the short data type. It has a value range of -32,768 to 32,767(inclusive). Its ranges include -32,768 and 32,767 respectively. Its initial value is 0\. Similar to how byte data types may be deployed to save memory, short data types can do the same. The size of integers are twice the size of shorts.

Example: short s = 10000, short r = -5000

*   int data type: A signed 32-bit twoʼs complement forms the int data type. Its range of values is between -2,147,483,648 (-2^(31)) and 2,147,483,647 (2^(31) -1)(inclusive). The number ranges between -2,147,483,648 and 2,147,483,647\. Its initial value is 0\. Unless there is no memory issue, the default data type for integral values is often the "int" data type.

Example: int a = 100000, int b = -200000

*   long data type: The long data type is a 64-bit complement integer. Its value range lies between -9,223,372,036,854,775,808 (-2^(63)) to 9,223,372,036,854,775,807 (2^(63 -1)) (inclusive). Its minimum value is - 9,223,372,036,854,775,808, and its maximum value is 9,223,372,036,854,775,807\. Its default value is 0\. The long data type is used when you need a range of values more than those provided by int.

Example: long a = 100000L, long b = -200000L

*   float data type: Single-precision 32-bit IEEE 754 floating point represents the float data type. If you want to save memory while working with huge floating point number arrays, it is advised to use a float (rather than a double). For exact quantities, such as monetary amounts, the float data type should never be employed. It is set at 0.0F by default.

Example: float f1 = 234.5f

*   double data type: A double-precision 64-bit IEEE 754 floating point represents the double data type. Like float, the double data type is frequently used for decimal numbers. Also, it is never a good idea to utilize the double data type for exact numbers like money. The default setting is 0.0d.

Example: double d1 = 12.3

[Default values of data type](#contents.xhtml_sc4_43a)

The assignment of a value at the time a field is specified is not always required. The compiler will assign fields to an appropriate default value if they are defined but not initialized. According to the data type, this default usually equals zero or null. However, it is typically thought of as poor programming practice to rely on such default settings. The default values of the different variables are given in [Table 2.4](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-4):

| Data Type | Default values |
| byte | 0 |
| short | 0 |
| int | 0 |
| long | 0L |
| float | 0.0f |
| double | 0.0d |
| char | ʼ\u0000ʼ |
| string | null |
| boolean | false |

Table 2.4: Default values of primitive data types

Let us discuss the various types of variables that can be possible in Java programming language.

[Types of variables in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc3_44a)

The Java programming language defines the following kinds of variables:

*   Instance variables (non-static fields): From the technological aspect (Object Oriented Programming), the objects store their individual states in fields as non-static, that is, fields not specified with the static keyword. They are considered instance variables because their values are unique to each instance of a class.

    class test{

    int var;

    }

    This type of variable is known as the instance variable. If we create several objects of the class test, each object will get its own copy. Let us suppose we have created three objects, t1, t2 and t3 using the following syntax.

    test t1=new test();

    test t2=new test();

    test t3=new test();

    The instance variables are represented in memory with their local copies of the variable vat, depicted in [Figure 2.3](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-3):

    ![](images/Figure_2.3.png)

    Figure 2.3: Instance variables

*   Class variables (Static fields): We can say that a class variable is any field with the static modifier prefixed before it. Also, there is exactly one copy of this variable available, irrespective of the number of objects of the class created, in other words, how many times the class has been instantiated.

    class test{

    static int var;

    }

    test t1=new test();

    test t2=new test();

    test t3=new test();

    The static variable (only one copy is created) is shared among all the objects created, and the concept is given in [Figure 2.4](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-4):

    ![](images/Figure_2.4.png)

    Figure 2.4: Static variable

*   Local variables: As an object stores its state in fields, local variables are used by a method to store its temporary state. An example of a local variable is void disp(int cnt).

    class test

    {

    void get_value(int x, int y)

    {

    }

    }

    In the preceding code, the variables x and y are known as local variables. The local variables are only visible to the methods in which they are declared; they are not accessible from the rest of the class. The rules for making proper variable names are given in the [Table 2.5](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-5):

| Rule no. | Meaning |
| 1 | The names of the variable names are case-sensitive in nature. |
| 2 | Any valid identifier beginning with a letter, the dollar sign "$", or the underscore character "_" can be used as the name of a variable. Also, it has an infinite length and consists of Unicode letters and numbers. |
| 3 | Although it is standard to use a letter rather than "$" or "_" to start variable names, this is not always the case. Additionally, it is conventional to never use the dollar sign symbol. |
| 4 | The dollar symbol can happen to be in automatically produced names, but it should never appear in variable names. Similar rules apply to the underscore character; although it is theoretically OK to start the name of your variable with "_", performing this is not recommended. There can be no white space. |
| 5 | Letters, numbers, dollar signs, and underscore characters are all acceptable as additional characters. This rule is also subject to conventions and common sense. |

Table 2.5: Rules for variable names

Instead of adopting confusing acronyms, use whole words when naming your variables. Your code will be simpler to read and comprehend if you do this. In many circumstances, it also makes your source code self-documenting; for instance, fields with names like name, age, and roll number are far more logical than those with abbreviations like nm, ae, and rl. The name you select must not be a keyword or a restricted word, as well.

If the name chosen by you is only one word, write that word in all lowercase letters. If it is created with more than one word, make the first letter a capital letter for each subsequent word. The names savings account and current account are the best examples, according to the specification. If a constant value is stored in your variable, such as static final int VALUE_PI = 3.142, the convention varies a little bit, making every letter capitalized and placing the underscore character "_" in between subsequent words. But the usage of the underscore character "_" is not encouraged anywhere.

In the next paragraph, a detailed discussion about the hierarchy of data types is given below with a diagram. In the next section, the concepts of type conversion and type casting are depicted with examples. In the next section, we will be discussing the concepts of type conversion and type casting with examples.

[Type casting and type conversion](#contents.xhtml_sc4_45a)

A cast is a way to inform the compiler that a conversion is going to be done, and data loss may be possible. If two types are compatible, Java will perform the conversion automatically. For example, it is always possible to assign an int value to a long variable. However, there is no automatic conversion defined from double to byte. To do this, you must explicitly convert the data type.

Automatic type conversion is done with two conditions:

*   The two types are compatible,
*   The destination type is larger than the source type.

If these two conditions are met, a widening operation takes place. The order is:

Byte | short | int | long | float | double, so we can convert an int (4 bytes) variable to a long (8 bytes) type variable. Also, the data type float can be easily converted into a double type variable.

If you want to assign an int (4 bytes) value to a byte (1 byte) variable, the conversion is known as narrowing conversion because byte is smaller than int. To create a conversion between incompatible types, you must use a cast in the following manner:

(target_type) value

Example:

public class typecn

{

public static void main(String s[])

{

int num=65;

double dnum=(double)num;

System.out.println(" int type is converted into double type "+" "+dnum);

}

}

In the preceding code, the variable num, which is of integer type, is converted into double and saved to dnum, which is a double variable. So, it is a simple example of the type conversion from data type int to double.

[Non-primitive data types or reference types](#contents.xhtml_sc2_46a)

Let us give a brief definition of different types of non-primitive data types available in Java:

*   String: The concept of Strings as a sequence of characters is widely used in Java programming. Strings are considered objects in Java.

    String msg = "A simple java program";

*   Array: Arrays are always assigned to a default value whether they are declared as instance variables or local variables. If the arrays are declared but not initialized, they will be assigned a default value of null.

    Example:

    int num_langs[];

    num_langs=new int[10];

*   Class: A class is actually a user-defined data type from which objects of a class can be created. The concept of class is developed to offer object-oriented properties like inheritance, polymorphism, and encapsulation. Generally, class models something in the actual world and consists of a set of variables that contain data and a set of methods that operate on the data.

    Example:

    class Point

    {

    int x;

    int y;

    void getCoordinate();

    }

*   Object: An object is known as an instance of a class. The variableʼs data type is technically the class from which it is constructed. It is stored as a pointer in some memory address. Until initialized using a new operator, it points to null.

    Example: Point p1=new Point();

*   Annotation: It allows you to link metadata (data about data) to the program components.

    *   In its simplest form, an annotation is represented as @Entity
    *   The at sign character (@) indicates to the compiler that what follows is an annotation. In the following example, the annotationʼs name is Override:

        @Override

        void mySupMethod() { ... }

    The annotation can include elements, which can be named or unnamed, and there are values for those elements:

    @Author(

    name = "Subhajit Adhikari",

    date = "29/07/2023"

    )

    Annotations can be applied to declarations: declarations of classes, fields, methods, and other program elements. When used on a declaration, each annotation often appears, by convention, on its own line.

*   Enumeration: A reference to a group of objects presenting a connected set of alternatives.

    Public enum Stream {

    CSE, IT, ME, EE, ECE, CE

    }

*   Interface: It offers a public API that is implemented by Java classes.

    interface Operatable {

    Boolean startTV( );

    void stopTV( );

    int volumeChange(int increment, int decrement);

    int channelChange(int nextCh);

    }

[Operators in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_47a)

The arithmetic operators, relational operators, bitwise operators, logical operators, assignment operators, and short circuit operators will be discussed in a tabular format with examples.

[Arithmetic operators](#contents.xhtml_sc3_48a)

Suppose two variables, S and T, are assigned values 7 and 5, respectively. Let us see [Table 2.6](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-6) below to understand the operator, the meaning of the operator, and the coding examples with output:

| Operator | Meaning of the Operator | Coding example | Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| + (addition) | Adds the values on either side of the operator | S+T | 12 |
| - (subtraction) | Subtracts right-hand operand from left-hand operand. | S-T | 2 |
| * (multiplication) | Multiply the values on either side of the operator | S*T | 35 |
| / (Division) | Divides right-hand operand from left-hand operand. | S/T | 1 |
| % (Modulus) | Divides right-hand operand by left-hand operand and returns the remainder. | S%T | 2 |
| ++ (increment) | Increment the value of the operand by 1 | S++ | 8 |
| -- (decrement) | Decrement the value of the operand by 1 | --T | 4 |

Table 2.6: Examples of Arithmetic operators

[Relational operators](#contents.xhtml_sc3_49a)

Assume variable S holds seven 7 and variable T holds 5\. The different relational operators have been explained with examples in [Table 2.7](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-7) below:

| Operator | Meaning of the Operator | Coding example | Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ==  (equal to) | Check if the values of two operands are equal or not. If yes, then the condition becomes true. | (S==T) | not true |
| !=  (not equal to) | Check if the values of two operands are equal or not. If values are not equal, then the condition becomes true. | (S!=T) | true |
| >  (greater than) | Checks if the value of the left operand is greater than the value of the right operand. If yes, then the condition becomes true. | (S>T) | true |
| <  (less than) | Check if the value of the left operand is less than the value of the right operand. If yes, then the condition becomes true. | (S<T) | false |
| >=  (greater than or equal to) | Checks if the value of the left operand is greater than or equal to the value of the right operand. If yes, then the condition becomes true. | (S>=T) | false |
| <=  (less than or equal to) | Checks if the value of the left operand is less than or equal to the value of the right operand. If yes, then the condition becomes true. | (S<=T) is | true |

Table 2.7: Examples of Relational operators

[Bitwise operators](#contents.xhtml_sc3_50a)

If a = 60 and b = 13, now in binary format, they will be as follows:

a = 0011 1100,

b = 0000 1101,

a&b = 0000 1100,

a|b = 0011 1101,

a^b = 0011 0001=49(decimal)

In [Table 2.8](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-8), all the bitwise operators have been discussed in detail:

| Operator | Meaning of the Operator | Coding Example | Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| &  (bitwise and) | The binary AND operator copies a bit to the result if it exists in both operands | a&b | 12 |
| &#124;  (bitwise or) | The binary OR operator copies a bit if it exists in both operands | a&#124;b | 61 |
| ^  (bitwise XOR) | The binary XOR operator copies a bit if it is set in both operands | a^b | 49 |
| ~  (bitwise compliment) | Binary 1ʼs compliment operator is unary and has the effect of flipping bits | ~a | -61 |
| <<  (left shift) | Binary left shift operator. The left operand value is moved left by the number of bits specified by the right operand. | a<<1 | 120 |
| >>  (right shift) | Binary right shift operator. The left operand value is moved right by the number of bits specified by the right operand. | b>>1 | 6 |
| >>>  (zero fill right shift) | Shift right zero fill operator. The left operand value is moved right by the number of bits specified by the right operand, and shifted values are filled up with zeros | b>>>1 | 6 |

Table 2.8: Examples of Bitwise operators

[Logical operators](#contents.xhtml_sc3_51a)

The following table lists the logical operators. Assume Boolean variable A holds true and variable B holds false, then A && B is false.

| Operator | Description | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| && (Logical and) | Returns true if both statements are true | (A && B ) is false |
| &#124;&#124; (Logical or) | Returns true if one of the statements is true | (A &#124;&#124; B ) is false |
| !(Logical not) | Reverses the result, returns false if the result is true | !(A && B ) is true |

Table 2.9: Examples of Logical operators

[Assignment operators](#contents.xhtml_sc3_52a)

Suppose we have three operands: A=10, B=20, and C=0. Let us discuss the assignment operators and how they work in [Table 2.10](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-10):

| Operator | Description | Example | Output |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| = | Simple assignment operator. Assigns values from right-side operands to left-side operands. | C=A+B | 30 |
| += | Add AND assignment operator. It adds the right operand to the left operand and assigns the result to the left operand. | C+=A is equivalent to C=C+A | 10 |
| -= | Subtract AND assignment operator. It subtracts the right operand from the left operand and assigns the result to the left operand. | C-=A is equivalent to C=C–A | -10 |
| *= | Multiply AND assignment operator. It multiplies the right operand with the left operand and assigns the result to the left operand. | C*=A is equivalent to C=C*A | 0 |
| /= | Divide AND assignment operator. It divides the left operand with the right operand and assigns the result to the left operand. | C/=A is equivalent to C=C/A | 0 |
| %= | Modulus AND assignment operator. It takes modulus using two operands and signs the result of the left operand. | C%=A is equivalent to C=C%A | 0 |
| <<= | Left shift AND assignment operator. | A<<=2issameasA=A<<2 | 40 |
| >>= | Right shift AND assignment operator. | A>>=2issameasA=A>>2 | 2 |
| &= | Bitwise AND assignment operator. | A&=2issameasA=A&2 | 2 |
| ^= | Bitwise exclusive OR and assignment operator. | A^=2issameasA=A^2 | 8 |
| &#124;= | Bitwise inclusive OR and assignment operator. | A&#124;=2issameasA=A&#124;2 | 10 |

Table 2.10: Examples of Assignment operators

[Short circuit operators](#contents.xhtml_sc4_53a)

The Logical AND (&&) and Logical OR (||)operators are short circuit operators. A short circuit operator is one that does not necessarily evaluate all of its operands.

Example 1

Suppose you have two operands, x and y, then x && y means y will be evaluated if x is true. In other words, if x is false, then y will not be evaluated. Let us consider the following code snippet of Logical AND operator (&&).

Example

if( 4==5 && 5+5==10 )

System.out.println("This line will not be printed.");

Explanation

*   The expression(4==5 && 5+5==10 ) is evaluated from left to right
*   Evaluate the expression 4 == 5, discovering that 4 == 5 is false
*   The output is false (without checking to check if 5 + 5 == 10)
*   Realize that the condition (4 == 5 && rest_part) must be false, no matter what the rest_part condition happens to be.

Note: When Java finds the value on the left side of an && operator to be false, then Java gives up and declares the entire expression to be false. That is called short circuit expression evaluation.

Example 2

Suppose you have two operands, x and y, then x || y means y will be evaluated. If  x is false, in other words, if x is true, then y won’t be evaluated. Let us consider the following code snippet of Logical OR operator (||)

Example

if( 4==4 || 5==6 )

System.out.println("This line will be printed.");

Explanation

*   when the value on the operatorʼs left side is true.
*   Evaluate the expression 4 == 4, finds that 4 == 4 is true. So, the output is true.
*   Realize that the condition (4 == 4 || rest_part) must be true, no matter what the rest_part condition happens to be.

After the discussion about the Java operators, we are going to discuss the operator precedence in Java in brief in the next section.

[Precedence of Java operators](#contents.xhtml_sc4_54a)

The different operators are listed in precedence order in [Table 2.11](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-11). The topmost operator has the highest precedence and then the second highest, and so on. The operators associated with higher precedence are examined before operators having lower precedence. The operatorʼs cell of the table is considered to have the same level of precedence. At the time of the appearance of equal precedence operators in the same expression, a rule must be followed.

All binary operators are evaluated from left to right. Only the assignment operators are evaluated as per right to left rule. The details are given in the [Figure 2.5](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-5).

![](images/Figure_2.5.png)

Figure 2.5: Operator precedence

[Conditional statements](#contents.xhtml_sc2_55a)

In the programs that we have examined to this point, each of the statements is executed once, in the order given. Most programs are more complicated because the sequence of statements and the number of times each is executed can vary. We use the term control flow to refer to statement sequencing in a program.

[If conditional statement](#contents.xhtml_sc3_56a)

The conditional sentence is the most fundamental of all control flow statements. It instructs your program to execute a certain chunk of code only if a specific test returns true. If this test returns false, control moves to the conclusion of the if-then expression. In [Figure 2.6](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-6), the flowchart of the if statement with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.6.png)

Figure 2.6: Flowchart of If statement with example

Syntax:

if (condition) {

// block of code to be executed if the condition is true

}

Note that if is in lowercase letters. Uppercase letters (If or IF) will generate an error.

In the example below, we test two values to find out if 20 is greater than 18\. If the condition is true, print some text:

Example

if (20 > 18) {

System.out.println("20 is greater than 18");

}

[If else condition](#contents.xhtml_sc3_57a)

When an if clause evaluates to false, an alternative path of execution is provided by the if-then-else statement. In [Figure 2.7](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-7), the flowchart of the if-else statement with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.7.png)

Figure 2.7: Flowchart of If-Else statement with example

Use the else statement to specify a block of code to be executed if the condition is false.

Syntax

if (condition) {

// block of code to be executed if the condition is true

} else {

// block of code to be executed if the condition is false

}

Example

int time = 20;

if (time < 18) {

System.out.println("Good day.");

} else {

System.out.println("Good evening.");

}

// Outputs "Good evening."

[Nested if condition](#contents.xhtml_sc3_58a)

When an if statement is used within another if statement, it is known as a nested if structure. In [Figure 2.8](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-8), the flowchart of nested if statement with example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.8.png)

Figure 2.8: Flowchart of nested If statement with example

Syntax:

if(expression_1) {

// Executes when the expression_1 is true

if(expression_2) {

// Executes when the expression_2 is true

}

}

Example

public class Test {

public static void main(String args[]) {

int x = 30;

int y = 10;

if( x == 30 ) {

System.out.print("X = 30");

if( y == 10 ) {

System.out.print("X = 30 and Y = 10");

}

}

System.out.print("Outside the nested if ");

}

}

[Nested if else statement](#contents.xhtml_sc3_59a)

The if-else ladder statement is a set of conditions that is based on the Nested if condition. The if-else ladder condition performs the expressions from top to bottom. Once the condition is true, the statement for the same is executed, and the balance ladder rules are skipped. In [Figure 2.9](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-9) and [Figure 2.10](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-10), the flowchart of nested if-else statements with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.9.png)

Figure 2.9: Flowchart of nested If-else statement

![](images/Figure_2.10.png)

Figure 2.10: Example of nested If-else statement

Syntax:

if (condition1) {

// block of code to be executed if condition1 is true

}

else if (condition2) {

// block of code to be executed if the condition1 is false and condition2 is true

} else {

// block of code to be executed if the condition1 is false and condition2 is false

}

Example:

int time = 22;

if (time < 10) {

System.out.println("Good morning.");

} else if (time < 18) {

System.out.println("Good day.");

} else {

System.out.println("Good evening.");

}

// Outputs "Good evening."

[Loops in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_60a)

We have presented the concept of conditional statements in Java and how they work. Now, the working procedure of the different loops is given in the next section. We are going to discuss the basic three loops for, while, and do-while loops. The concept of loop is used to do the same work in terms of code blocks repeatedly until the specified condition is false.

[For loop](#contents.xhtml_sc3_61a)

When you know exactly how many times you want to loop through a block of code, use the for loop instead of a while loop. Iterating over a range of values efficiently is made possible by the for loop statement. Since the loop iterates continuously until a certain condition is met, programmers frequently refer to it as the for loop. In [Figure 2.11](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-11), the flowchart of the for loop with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.11.png)

Figure 2.11: Flowchart of for Loop with example

Syntax:

for (statement 1; statement 2; statement 3) {

// code block to be executed

}

Explanation of for loop:

1.  statement 1: The initialization statement will be executed for once when the loop begins.
2.  statement 2: The statement of condition will be checked throughout the loop. If true, then statements inside the loop will be executed again and again until the condition becomes false
3.  statement 3: The statement of iteration will be executed and return the control to step 2.

[Labeled for loop](#contents.xhtml_sc4_62a)

A valid variable name that indicates the name of the loop to which execution control should go is called a label. The example of labeled for loop in Java is given below:

public class lb_loop

{

public static void main(String args[])

{

m:

for(int i=0;i<5;i++)

{

n:

for(int j=0;j<5;j++)

{

if(j==2)

{

System.out.println("Exit from the loop labeled as m");

break m;

}

}

}

}

}

[While loop](#contents.xhtml_sc3_63a)

The while loop evaluates the expression, which must yield a Boolean value. If the expression qualifies as true, the while loop executes the statement(s) in the while block. The while statement continues to test the expression and execute its block until the expression returns false. In [Figure 2.12](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-12), the flowchart of the while loop with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.12.png)

Figure 2.12: Flowchart of While Loop with example

Syntax:

while (condition) {

// code block to be executed

}

In the following example, the code in the loop will run over and over again as long as a variable (i) is less than 5:

Example:

int i = 0;

while (i< 5) {

System.out.println(i);

i++;

}

[Do while loop](#contents.xhtml_sc3_64a)

The do-while loop is a variant of the while loop. This loop will execute the code block once before checking if the condition is true, then it will repeat the loop as long as the condition is true. In [Figure 2.13](#ch02.xhtml_fig2-13), the flowchart of the do-while loop with an example is given:

![](images/Figure_2.13.png)

Figure 2.13: Flowchart of Do-While Loop with example

Syntax:

do {

// code block to be executed

}

while (condition);

The following example uses a do…while loop. The loop will always be executed at least once, even if the condition is false because the code block is executed before the condition is tested:

Example:

int i = 0;

do {

System.out.println(i);

i++;

}

while (i< 5);

[Comparison between for, while, and do-while loops](#contents.xhtml_sc4_65a)

We have learned the syntax and coding examples of for, while, and do-while loops in detail with the help of flowcharts in the previous section. Now, in [Table 2.11](#ch02.xhtml_tab2-11), the comparison between the basic three loops, for, while, and do-while, is presented:

| For loop | While loop | Do-While loop |
| It is used when the number of iterations is known. | It is used when the number of iterations is not known. | It is when the number of iterations is not known. |
| The loop body will not be executed if the condition is false | The loop body will not be executed if the condition is false | The loop body will be executed once, even if the condition is false |
| It is a better choice when elements of the collection, like data structures like an array, are processed. | It is used when you take input from a command line or a specific value from a method call. | It is used to take input from the user and then check whether the condition is true or false. |
| In case of no condition, the loop is repeated infinite times. | In case of no condition, an error will be shown. | In case of no condition, an error will be shown. |
| Initialization can be in or out of the loop | Initialization is always out of the loop | Initialization is always out of the loop |
| It is known as the pretest loop | It is known as the pretest loop | It is known as the post-test loop |

Table 2.11: Comparison between For, While and Do-while Loop

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_66a)

In this chapter, the basic preliminaries like tokens, data types, type casting, operators, conditional statements, and loops have been covered with flowchart representation and coding examples. These programming constructs help the readers enhance their knowledge of writing Java programs and applications from scratch. A brief introduction to non-primitive data types like class, string, array, enum, and many more have been presented. Now, with the help of these basic building blocks of Java programming, we move forward to discuss the concepts of class, objects, and methods in the upcoming chapter.

    3.  &&,||
    4.  /,*
10.  If a = 60 and b = 13 , find the value of a^b, where ^is a bitwise operator.
    1.  12
    2.  49
    3.  -49
    4.  61

[Answers](#contents.xhtml_sc3_69a)

1.  c
2.  d
3.  c
4.  c
5.  b
6.  a
7.  a
8.  c
9.  c
10.  b
