# [CHAPTER 5Types of Inheritance in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch05a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_142a)

In the previous chapter, the study of arrays, string and vectors has been discussed in detail. Now, we will focus on the concepts of inheritance and the need of heritance along with the different types of inheritance. Then, we will cover the most important concept related to inheritance, is method overriding and how it is achieved with programs. Also, the usage of super keyword is added with different contexts. The functionality of the final variable, class and methods to restrict the subclass to inherit the properties and behaviors of the base class, will be studied in this chapter. The concept of interfaces will be presented to achieve multiple inheritance in Java. Last, but not the least, the different types of access modifiers are given with code and diagrams for better understanding of the learners.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_143a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Introduction to inheritance
*   Need for inheritance
*   Types of inheritance in Java
*   Advantages of inheritance
*   Method overriding
*   Dynamic method dispatch
*   Super keyword
*   Final keyword
*   Abstract class
*   Interface in Java
*   Access modifiers

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_144a)

The primary objective of this chapter is to provide the concept of how code is reused in terms of inheritance. The need of inheritance will be discussed with the aim to discuss code reusability. Then with different types of inheritance will be presented with proper diagrams and source codes. Next, the idea of method overriding is presented with example. It is required for how a same method behaves in different situations differently. The most important concept Dynamic Method Dispatch regarding method overriding is explained with proper example. Then, how the keyword final is used to protect the inheritance in different ways, is demonstrated. With the help of final class, final method and final data members, the inheritance property can be restricted. Thus, learners will learn how to restrict the inheritance property using the concept of final keyword. This chapter will also deliver the brief idea about abstract class and how it is implemented in Java for acting as base class only. In this chapter, also we will deliver the concept of inheritance, and how it helps to implement multiple inheritance in Java. Last but not the list the idea of access modifiers available in Java will be discussed with pictorial representation and examples.

[Introduction to inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc2_145a)

In this section, we are going to introduce the concept of inheritance with a very simple example. Now, as per the definition, we can say that when one object acquires all the properties and behaviors of a parent object, it is known as inheritance. It provides code reusability. In the following [Figure 5.1](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-1), a simple code is given as an example:

![](images/Figure_5.1.png)

Figure 5.1: Example of inheritance

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.2.png)

Figure 5.2: Output of the example of inheritance

In the preceding [Figure 5.1](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-1), we are trying to present a very basic real-life example as we all inherit a surname from our parents. In the given example, the surname is taken as lname as a String, which is defined in the base class father. The derived class chld inherits the property lname from the base class having one property as fname. Then creating the object c1 of the chld class we are displaying the name of the child as SubhajitAdhikari.

Next, the following figure represents the terms used in inheritance:

![](images/Figure_5.3.png)

Figure 5.3: Terns used in inheritance

As we can see in [Figure 5.3](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-3), a child inherits the properties from his father. Similarly, in Java, there are two classes, such as:

*   Parent class (Super or Base class)
*   Child class (Subclass or Derived class)

A class which inherits the properties is known as child class, whereas a class whose properties are inherited is known as parent class using extend keyword. In the following [Figure 5.4](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-4), the concept is demonstrated:

![](images/Figure_5.4.png)

Figure 5.4: Example of inheritance with syntax in Java

In the preceding [Figure 5.4](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-4), there are two classes, Base class and Derived class. The syntax is also provided on how inheritance can be implemented in Java programming. The keyword extends is used by the Derived class to inherit properties and behaviors of the Base class. In the next section, the important terms used in Inheritance are discussed.

[Terms used in inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc3_146a)

The following terminologies are used best to describe inheritance:

*   Sub class: Subclass is a class which inherits the other class. It is also called a derived class, extended class, or child class.
*   Super class: Super class is the class from where a subclass inherits the features. It is also called a base class or a parent class.

The extends keyword extends a class (indicates that a class is inherited from another class). Now, the need for the inheritance property in OOP is discussed in the next section.

[Need for inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc2_147a)

The first reason for the need of inheritance is that when a class is merely a modification of another class, there is minimal repetition of the same code. The next reason is that the localization of code can be achieved through inheritance in the form of the following:

*   If you are fixing a bug in the parent class, it will automatically fix it in the child class.
*   If you add functionality in the parent class, it will automatically add it in the child classes.
*   There are less chances of different and inconsistent implementations of the same operation.

After having a detailed discussion of the basic concepts of inheritance, we are now going to understand the different kinds of inheritance that are possible in Java programming language.

[Types of inheritance in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_148a)

There are four types of inheritance possible in Java, such as:

*   Single
*   Multi-level
*   Hierarchical
*   Multiple (achieved through interface)

In this section, we will discuss the first three basic types of inheritance along with their pictorial representation. You can see the source codes in [Figure 5.5](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-5):

![](images/Figure_5.5.png)

Figure 5.5: Example of different types of inheritance

[Single inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc3_149a)

When a single class gets derived from its base class, then this type of inheritance is termed as single inheritance. In single inheritance, only one base class and one child class will be allowed. The concept is explained in [Figure 5.5](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-5). The source code is provided in the following [Figure 5.6](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-6):

![](images/Figure_5.6.png)

Figure 5.6: Example of single inheritance

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.7.png)

Figure 5.7: Output of the single inheritance

In the [Figure 5.6](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-6), we have taken two classes out of which one represents the base class as class A and one represents the derived class B. There is only one property or data member of the base class A which is of String type st and the value is assigned as Fish is inherited in derived class B. In the method think(), which is defined in derived class B, the data member st is reused using the concept of inheritance. As a result, the output is coming as A is eating Fist, and B is thinking about Fish, one after one.

In the next section, multi-level inheritance is presented with code.

[Multi-level inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc3_150a)

A derived class is created from another derived class in this type of inheritance and can have any number of levels. Multilevel inheritance is the process of deriving a class from another class that itself was descended from another class, that is, a class with more than one parent class but descended from them at different levels. The example is provided in the following [Figure 5.8](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-8):

![](images/Figure_5.8.png)

Figure 5.8: Example of multi-level inheritance

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.9.png)

Figure 5.9: Output of multi-level inheritance

In the preceding [Figure 5.8](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-8), multi-level inheritance is implemented using three classes A, B and C. The behavior or method like eat() of class A is inherited by class B. The behavior or method like think() of class B is inherited by class C. So, in class C all the methods or behaviors are available to use and that is why using the object of class C, we can invoke the methods using the object c available in other levels of classes.

Hierarchical inheritance is one of the most important type of inheritance which is outlined in the next section.

[Hierarchical inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc3_151a)

Multiple child classes can inherit the methods and attributes of a single parent class using a kind of inheritance called hierarchical inheritance. Due to hierarchical inheritance, child classes do not need to worry about individually developing the methods and properties that the parent class has access to. Instead, they only need to construct the methods and properties that are special to their class. As a result, code redundancy is also decreased because it is rarely duplicated. So, in the end, hierarchical inheritance makes the code more reusable, less redundant, modular, and understandable. The example is provided in the following [Figure 5.10](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-10):

![](images/Figure_5.10.png)

Figure 5.10: Example of hierarchical inheritance

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.11.png)

Figure 5.11: Output of hierarchical inheritance

In the preceding example [Figure 5.10](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-10), the method eat() is available in all the derived classes like B and C. We can invoke this method using the objects of B and C, thus the output A is eating is coming twice. Also class C has one new method that is join() , we also can also invoke it, as per the concept of inheritance. Let us discuss the advantages of inheritance.

[Advantages of inheritance](#contents.xhtml_sc2_152a)

There are three basic advantages of inheritance, such as:

*   Code reusability: Using inheritance, the public methods of base class can be reused in derived classes.
*   Data hiding: The private data of base class cannot be altered by derived class
*   Overriding: With inheritance, we will be able to override the methods of the base class in the derived class

After the detailed discussion about inheritance, the most relevant concept method overriding is discussed in the next section.

[Method overriding](#contents.xhtml_sc2_153a)

If the same method with same signature is defined in both the super class and the subclass, it is known as method overriding. It will always present the sub class method version, at the time of calling from sub class. Suppose we know that Bank is a class that provides functionality to get the rate of interest. However, the rate of interest varies according to different banks. SBI, ICICI, or AXIS banks could provide 8%, 7%, or 3% rate of interest. In this way, suppose the method calInterest() can be overridden into three classes, SBI, ICICI, and AXIS, with the same name and signature to provide different rate of interest. Now, let us discuss the rules for overriding.

[Rules of method overriding](#contents.xhtml_sc3_154a)

The following are the rules of method overriding:

*   Method name should be same
*   Argument list should be same
*   Implementation is different.
*   Return type of methods are same.

The example of method overriding is provided in the following [Figure 5.12](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-12):

![](images/Figure_5.12.png)

Figure 5.12: Example of method overriding

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.13.png)

Figure 5.13: Output of method overriding

In the preceding example in [Figure 5.12](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-12), the method disp() has the same name and signature defined separately in base class A and sub class B. At the time of method calls using the instance of sub class B, that is objB, it always overrides the method of super class. This is the reason why the output is In class B, which is a version of sub class not the super class version of method disp().

Now, we will discuss the concept of Dynamic Method Dispatch outlined with an example.

[Dynamic method dispatch](#contents.xhtml_sc2_155a)

A super class reference variable can refer to a subclass object. At runtime, Java uses this fact to resolve calls to overridden methods. Java selects which version of that method to execute based on the type of object being referenced to at the moment the call happens at run time. When multiple types of objects are referenced, different versions of an overridden method are called. The example of dynamic method dispatch is provided in the following [Figure 5.14](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-14):

![](images/Figure_5.14.png)

Figure 5.14: Example of dynamic method dispatch

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.15.png)

Figure 5.15: Output of dynamic method dispatch

In the preceding program in [Figure 5.14](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-14), creates one super class called A and two subclasses of it, called B and C. Subclasses B and C override disp() declared in A. Inside the main() method, objects of type A, B, and C are declared. Also, a reference of type A, called r, is declared. The program then assigns a reference to each type of object to r and uses that reference to invoke disp(). As the output shows, the version of disp() executed is determined by the type of object being referred to at the time of the call. Had it been determined by the type of the reference variable, r, you would see three calls to A objectʼs disp() method.

Now, let us briefly discuss the usage of super keyword in next paragraph.

[Super keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc2_156a)

If your method overrides one of its super classʼs methods, you can invoke the overridden method through the use of the keyword super. You can also use super to refer to a hidden field (although hiding fields is discouraged). It can be used to refer immediate parent class method. The example is provided in [Figure 5.16](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-16):

![](images/Figure_5.16.png)

Figure 5.16: Example of super keyword

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.17.png)

Figure 5.17: Output of super keyword

In the preceding [Figure 5.16](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-16), the source code of super keyword is given. We can see that it will call immediate parent class A method disp() using the code super.disp() in child class B. So, the output is Inside class B with Inside class A one after another.

Another application of super is to invoke a super classʼs constructor. The syntax for calling a super class constructor is provided in [Figure 5.18](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-18). However, it must be the first line of code inside the child class constructor:

![](images/Figure_5.18.png)

Figure 5.18: Example of super keyword to invoke parent class constructor

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.19.png)

Figure 5.19: Output of Example of super keyword to invoke parent class constructor

From the preceding [Figure 5.18](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-18), we can observe that invocation of a super class constructor super() must be the first line in the subclass constructor. In child class B, inside the constructor B(), super() must be the first line and the second line of code can be anything like String s="hii.

Now, let us discuss the concept of final keyword in the next section.

[Final keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc2_157a)

For classes, attributes, and methods, the final keyword is a non-access modifier that prevents changes to them (impossible to inherit or override). When a variable, like PI, should always store the same value, the final keyword comes in handy (3.14159).

The usage of final keyword with respect to variable, class and method is discussed in the following sections.

[Final variable](#contents.xhtml_sc3_158a)

If you make any variable as final, you cannot change the value of final variable. It will be constant. The example is provided in the following [Figure 5.20](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-20):

![](images/Figure_5.20.png)

Figure 5.20: Example of final variable

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.21.png)

Figure 5.21: Output of final variable

[Final class](#contents.xhtml_sc3_159a)

A final class cannot be extended to create a subclass. All methods in a final class are implicitly final. Class String is an example of a final class. The example is provided in the following [Figure 5.22](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-22):

![](images/Figure_5.22.png)

Figure 5.22: Example of final class

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.23.png)

Figure 5.23: Output of final class

You might want to do this for the following reasons:

*   To increase system security by preventing system subversion, and
*   For reasons of good object-oriented design.
*   Security: Making a subclass of a class and then replacing the original class with it is one method that hackers use to undermine systems. Although the subclass has a similar appearance and feel to the original class, it operates very differently and may even inflict harm or access personal data. You may declare your class to be final to stop this form of subversion and stop the creation of any subclasses as a result. For precisely this reason, the String class is a final class. This class must ensure that every time a method or object uses a String, it receives an exact Java.lang.String and not another sort of string because it is so essential to the Java platformʼs functionality. This guarantees that no string possesses any peculiar, inconsistent, unwanted, or unexpected characteristics.

    The compiler refuses to build your program and produces an error message if you attempt to compile a subclass of a final class. Additionally, the Java runtime system makes sure that bytecode-level subversion does not occur. By making sure that a class is not a subclass of a final class, it accomplishes this.

*   Design: A class might also need to be declared final for object-oriented design purposes. You could believe that your class is perfect or that it should not have any subclasses at all.

[Final method](#contents.xhtml_sc3_160a)

All or a portion of a classʼs methods might be designated as final. If a method cannot be overridden by subclasses, it is indicated by the final keyword in the method definition. This is done via the Object class, which has a few final methods. A methodʼs implementation should not be altered, and it is essential to maintaining the objectʼs consistency, you may want to declare it final. The example is provided in the following [Figure 5.24](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-24):

![](images/Figure_5.24.png)

Figure 5.24: Example of final method

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.25.png)

Figure 5.25: Output of final method

[Abstract class](#contents.xhtml_sc2_161a)

A class that has been declared abstract is one that may or may not have abstract methods. Although they cannot be created from scratch, abstract classes can be subclassed. When a subclass of an abstract class is created, it typically offers implementations for each of the parent classʼs abstract methods. The example is the following [Figure 5.26](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-26):

![](images/Figure_5.26.png)

Figure 5.26: Example of abstract class

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.27.png)

Figure 5.27: Output of abstract class

Abstract class have the following properties:

*   An abstract class must be declared with an abstract keyword.
*   It can have abstract and non-abstract methods.
*   It cannot be instantiated.
*   They cannot be used by a standalone class
*   It is used only used for inheritance
*   Abstract methods cannot have a body
*   An abstract method is a method that is declared without an implementation (without braces, and followed by a semicolon)
*   Abstract method must be defined in the subclass
*   It can have constructors and static methods also.
*   It can have final methods which will force the subclass not to change the body of the method.

Now, we demonstrate that the abstract class can have the constructor with an example in [Figure 5.28](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-28):

![](images/Figure_5.28.png)

Figure 5.28: Example of abstract class with constructor

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.29.png)

Figure 5.29: Output of abstract class with constructor

In the preceding code in [Figure 5.28](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-28), using the constructor A() of the abstract class A, we can assign a value of the variable a using this.a and that can be used to make a simple multiplication. In child class, using the super(2) we will pass the value of the variable as 2. Then in the method multiply(), the actual multiplication takes place in form of this.a * val, where the variable val is initialized to 3 using the statement System.out.println("The multiplied value is "+a.multiply(3)) in the method main(). So, the output is coming as The multiplied value is 6. Now, we will demonstrate the abstract class having static method with code in the [Figure 5.30](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-30):

![](images/Figure_5.30.png)

Figure 5.30: Example of abstract class with static method

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.31.png)

Figure 5.31: Output of abstract class with static method

In [Figure 5.30](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-30), there is one abstract class Shape with one static method disp(), the child class CShape is inheriting the properties of base class Shape. In the child class, using the statement super.disp(), we can call the method disp() present in parent class Shape. Thus, the output is The color of the shape is Blue which is written in the static method disp() in parent class Shape.

In this context we will also present the difference between abstract and non-abstract method in the next section.

[Difference between non abstract and abstract method](#contents.xhtml_sc3_162a)

There are significant differences between non abstract method and abstract method. They are tabulated in [Table 5.1](#ch05.xhtml_tab5-1):

| Non abstract method | Abstract method |
| It has the body | It does not contain the body |
| It is complete | It is not complete |
| The body is written within a class. | The body is written in the child class. |
| Example:void disp(){System.out.println("Welcome");} | Example:abstract void disp(); |

Table 5.1: Difference between abstract and non-abstract methods

After the discussion of inheritance property, final keyword and abstract class, this is the perfect segment to introduce the concept of interface in details.

[Interface in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_163a)

By the methods they expose, objects specify how they interact with the outside world. The interface between a thing and the outside world is formed by methods. For instance, the buttons on the front of your television serve as a point of contact between you and the electrical wires on the other side of the deviceʼs plastic casing. To turn the television on and off, use the power button. An interface, in its most basic form, is a collection of connected methods with empty bodies.

When a class implements an interface, it may be more formal about the behavior it guarantees to deliver. The contract that interfaces create between a class and the outside world is upheld at build time by the compiler. Before your class to properly compiles, all of the methods described by the interface your class claims can implement must be present in the classʼs source code.

With its interfaces system, Java builds on the abstract method notion. There are times when it is preferable to only supply the prototypes of a group of techniques. An interface is what is used for this in Java. A classʼs behavior, including some or all of its methods, is defined by an interface. A Java class can claim to implement an interface and then go about adding the necessary methods. The inheritance structure or the implementation used by a class that implements an interface are not required to come from any specific places.

An interface declaration has two components: the interface keyword and the interface name. The interface can be used by any class in any package, according to the public access specifier. Your interface will only be accessible to classes that are declared in the same package as it if you do not indicate that it is public. An additional element that can be included in an interface definition is a list of super interfaces. The structure is provided in [Figure 5.32](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-32):

![](images/Figure_5.32.png)

Figure 5.32: Syntax of interface

A class can extend or subclass another class, similarly to how an interface can extend other interfaces. Whereas an interface can extend unlimited number of interfaces, a class can only extend one other class. All the interfaces that the new interface extends are listed in the list of super interfaces, which is separated by commas. In [Figure 5.33](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-33), the different ways of implementing interfaces along with extending classes are given:

![](images/Figure_5.33.png)

Figure 5.33: Example of interfaces

We have an idea how the interfaces are implemented along with extending classes from [Figure 5.33](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-33). Some of them are as follows:

*   One class can implement one interface.
*   One interface can extend one interface.
*   One class can implement more than one interface.
*   One interface can extend more than one interface.
*   One class can implement more than one interface.

All of the methods included in the interfaceʼs body are declared in its method declarations. As an interface does not offer implementations for the methods stated inside it, a semicolon (;) is placed after the method declaration. Any method that is declared in an interface is inherently public and abstract. Together with method declarations, an interface may also have constant declarations. Every constant value declared in an interface is inherently public, static, and final. Certain declaration modifiers, such as transitory, volatile, and synchronized, are prohibited from usage in member declarations of an interface. Moreover, you cannot declare an interfaceʼs members using the private and protected specifiers.

The features of interface are listed as follows:

*   It is used for declaration purpose
*   Data members are static and final
*   Methods are abstract in nature
*   It specifies what a class must do but not how it does it
*   Any number of classes can implement an interface
*   One class can implement any number of interfaces
*   One interface can extend more than one interface

[Multiple inheritance using interfaces](#contents.xhtml_sc3_164a)

Java supports multiple inheritance only using interfaces. The coding example is provided in the following [Figure 5.34](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-34):

![](images/Figure_5.34.png)

Figure 5.34: Example of Multiple Inheritance using Interfaces

The following is the output of the preceding code:

![](images/Figure_5.35.png)

Figure 5.35: Output of multiple inheritance using interfaces

In the preceding code in [Figure 5.34](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-34), the statement class C implements I, J can be a valid statement , if I and J are the two interfaces. Thus, multiple interfaces can be implemented by a class, supporting the concept of multiple inheritance in Java. The properties and behaviors of interface I and J can be available in class C, so the method void f() and void f1() is defined in class C as child class. Now using the object obj of class C, we can invoke the methods f void f() and void f1() to print the values in the output like The value of a is 10 and The value of b is 20 one after another.

[Difference between abstract class and interface](#contents.xhtml_sc3_165a)

Let us discuss the difference between abstract class and interface as shown in [Table 5.2](#ch05.xhtml_tab5-2):

| Abstract class | Interface |
| The abstract keyword is used to declare abstract class. | The interface keyword is used to declare interface. |
| Abstract class can have abstract and non-abstract methods and also can have default and static methods also. | Interface can have only abstract methods |
| An abstract class may contain non-final variables. | Variables are by default final |
| Abstract class can be extended with keyword extends. | Interface can be implemented using keyword implements |
| A Java abstract class can have class members like private, protected, etc. | Members of a Java interface are public by default. |
| An abstract class can extend another Java class and implement multiple Java interfaces. | An interface can extend another Java interface only. |
| It doesnʼt support multiple inheritance. | It supports multiple inheritance. |

Table 5.2: Difference between abstract class and interface

Let us move to the topics of access modifiers in Java in the next section.

[Access modifiers](#contents.xhtml_sc2_166a)

Access level modifiers determine whether other classes can use a particular field or invoke a particular method. There are two levels of access control:

*   At the top level: public, or package-private (no explicit modifier).
*   At the member level: public, private, protected, or package-private (no explicit modifier).

A class may be declared with the modifier public, in which case that class is visible to all classes everywhere. If a class has no modifier (the default, also known as package-private), it is visible only within its own package (packages are named groups of related classes — you will learn about them in a later lesson.)

At the member level, you can also use the public modifier or no modifier (package-private) just as with top-level classes, and with the same meaning. For members, there are two additional access modifiers: private and protected. The private modifier specifies that the member can only be accessed in its own class. The protected modifier specifies that the member can only be accessed within its own package (as with package-private) and, in addition, by a subclass of its class in another package. The [Table 5.3](#ch05.xhtml_tab5-3) will provide the details of the access specifiers according to the package structure.

|  | PUBLIC | PROTECTED | DEFAULT | PRIVATE |
| Same class | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Sub Class in same package | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Other classes in same package | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Sub Class in other package | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Non-subclass in other package | Yes | No | No | No |

Table 5.3: Difference types of access modifiers

[Example of access modifiers](#contents.xhtml_sc3_167a)

In this section, we are presenting different access modifiers like public, protected, default, private protected, and private access modifiers with pictures.

![](images/Figure_5.36.png)

Figure 5.36: Example of public access modifier

In the [Figure 5.36](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-36), we can observe that the properties of the Base class can be accessed either by sub class or non-sub class from inside same package and also in the other package.

![](images/Figure_5.37.png)

Figure 5.37: Example of protected access modifier

In the [Figure 5.37](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-37), we can observe that the properties of the Base class can be accessed either by sub class or non-sub class from inside the same package but cannot be accessed by non-sub class in the other package.

![](images/Figure_5.38.png)

Figure 5.38: Example of default access modifier

In the [Figure 5.38](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-38), we can observe that the properties of the Base class can be accessed either by sub class or non-sub class from inside the same package but cannot be accessed by sub class or non-sub class in other package.

![](images/Figure_5.39.png)

Figure 5.39: Example of private protected access modifier

In the [Figure 5.39](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-39), we can observe that the properties of the Base class can be accessed only by sub class in the same package but cannot be accessed by non-sub class in same package and not accessed by sub class or non-sub class in other package:

![](images/Figure_5.40.png)

Figure 5.40: Example of private access modifier

In the [Figure 5.40](#ch05.xhtml_fig5-40), we can observe that the properties of the Base class can be accessed only inside the class irrespective of any other package.

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_168a)

In this chapter, we are trying to cover a lot of important concepts of OOPs like inheritance, method overriding, dynamic method dispatch, the super keyword, final keyword, abstract class, interface, and access modifiers with a variety of examples and diagrams. This chapter will inform the readers about the need of inheritance property. Also, if required using final keyword, users can restrict this property to be used. Important concepts, like abstraction can be understood using abstract class and interface with detailed analysis. The most important concept of OOP, Access modifiers and the actual meaning of the different access modifiers are presented with a table. At last, using diagrams the concept of access modifiers are redefined for the readerʼs better understandability and its application.

In the next chapter, the concept of multi-threading will be demonstrated. The way of thread creation is represented. The thread priority will also be discussed in the next chapter.

    1.  Base class can be accessed by sub class and non-sub class in same package
    2.  Base class can be accessed by sub class in other package
    3.  Base class can be accessed by non-sub class in other package
    4.  All of these

# [CHAPTER 6Concept of Thread in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch06a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_173a)

In this chapter, the concept of multi-threading is placed in such a way that everyone can learn multi-threading to develop real-life applications. Thread lifecycle is also presented in a diagram with brief description. Different ways of creating thread in Java are included with proper examples. The detailed discussion of thread class and runnable interface is one of the most important parts of this chapter along with thread priority. Important methods like join(), and isAlive() associated with thread programming have been elaborated in this chapter. Last but not least, the concepts of thread synchronization and inter thread communication have been added to this chapter.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_174a)

In this chapter, the following topics are discussed:

*   Multi thread programming
*   Thread lifecycle
*   Thread priority
*   Benefits of multi-threading
*   Important methods of thread programming
*   Thread synchronization
*   Inter-thread communication

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_175a)

Introducing the concept of multi-threading is the first objective of this chapter. The way multi-threading can be coded is presented here. The second objective is to give proper idea of thread life cycle combined with programs. Next, the process of thread creation is in the queue of objectives. Additionally, the thread class and runnable interface are presented with codes. Then how priorities can be set to the individual threads is included. The benefits of multi-threading is also a matter of concern in this chapter along with two most important concepts like thread synchronization and inter process communication are presented one after another. The real word applications are heavily dependent on the concept of multi-threading and thus we are concentrating about each of the building blocks of multi-threading using Java in this chapter.

Now, let us start our discussion with the concept of multi-threading

[Multi-thread programming](#contents.xhtml_sc2_176a)

The process of running many threads at once is known as multithreading in Java. The smallest processing unit is a thread, which is a lightweight sub-process. To accomplish multitasking, both multiprocessing and multithreading are utilized. As the threads employ a shared memory space, multithreading is preferred over multiprocessing. Saving memory and reducing the amount of time it takes to transition between threadsʼ contexts are two benefits of not allocating a distinct memory region. Most games, animation, and so on, employ Java multithreading. Suppose the authority wants to build a LAB for Java. To do that, a developer, painter, electricians are required to do three different jobs, namely:

*   Developing
*   Painting
*   Electrical wiring

So, we can consider each jobs as an independent piece of work. It is called threads. In programming, thread is a lightweight process. It can be thought of as independent flow of a program, as shown in the following figure:

![](images/Figure_6.1.png)

Figure 6.1: Example of multi-threading

The concept of thread lifecycle is explained in the next segment.

[Thread lifecycle](#contents.xhtml_sc2_177a)

A thread can be in only one state at a given point in time. A thread can be in one of the following states:

*   NEW: A thread that has not yet started is in this state.
*   RUNNABLE: A thread executing in the Java virtual machine is in this state. Thread state for a runnable thread. A thread in the runnable state is executing in the Java virtual machine but it may be waiting for other resources from the operating system such as processor.
*   BLOCKED: This condition refers to a thread that is stalled while awaiting a monitor lock. Blocking while awaiting a monitor lock is the thread state. A thread that is stalled is waiting for a monitor lock before it may enter a synchronized block or function or enter it again after calling Object. wait.
*   WAITING: A thread that is waiting indefinitely for another thread to perform a particular action is in this state.
*   TIMED_WAITING: A thread that is waiting for another thread to perform an action for up to a specified waiting time is in this state.
*   TERMINATED: A thread that has exited is in this state.

The thread lifecycle is elaborated in the following [Figure 6.2](#ch06.xhtml_fig6-2):

![](images/Figure_6.2.png)

Figure 6.2: Thread life cycle

The process of thread creation is outlined in the next section.

[Creating threads](#contents.xhtml_sc3_178a)

To create a thread, instantiation of an object of thread class is used. However, Java defines two ways in which this can be accomplished:

*   You can implement the runnable interface
*   You can extend the thread class itself

First, the thread creation using runnable interface is presented.

[Thread creation using Runnable interface](#contents.xhtml_sc4_179a)

To implement Runnable, a class need only implement a single method called run(), which is declared like this: public void run(). Inside run(), you will define the code that constitutes the new thread, mentioned as follows:

class TstThrd1 implements Runnable{

public void run(){

System.out.println("TstThrd1 is running");

}

In the preceding coding example, after you create a class TstThrd1 that implements Runnable, you will instantiate an object of type Thread  from within that class. Thread defines several constructors. The one that we will use is shown as follows:

Thread(Runnable threadOb): In this constructor, threadOb is an instance of a class that implements the Runnable interface. This defines where execution of the thread will begin.

TstThrd1 th2=new TstThrd1();

Thread t1 =new Thread(th2);

After the new thread is created, it will not start running until you call its start() method, which is declared within Thread. In essence, start() executes a call to run(). The start() method is invoked as t1.start(); In the following table, the process is presented:

class TstThrd1 implements Runnable

{

public void run()

{

System.out.println("TstThrd1 is running");

}

public static void main(String args[])

{

TstThrd1 th2=new TstThrd1();

Thread t1 =new Thread(th2);

t1.start();

}

}

Output:

![](images/Figure_6.3.png)

Figure 6.3: Example of runnable interface

In the next section, we will cover how thread is created using extending the thread class is outlined with example.

[Extending thread](#contents.xhtml_sc4_180a)

The second way to create a thread is to create a new class that extends Thread, and then to create an instance of that class. The extending class must override the run() method, which is the entry point for the new thread. It must also call start() to begin execution of the new thread. In [Table 6.1](#ch06.xhtml_tab6-1), the process is demonstrated:

| import java.lang.Thread; |
| class Thrd1 extends Thread{public void run() {for(inti = 1; i<=5; i++) {System.out.println("Thread1" + " "+i);}}} | class Thrd2 extends Thread{public void run(){for(int j = 1; j <=5; j++){System.out.println("Thread2" + " "+j);}}} | class Thrd3 extends Thread{public void run(){for(int k = 1; k <=5; k++){System.out.println("Thread3" + " "+k);}}} |
| public class TstThrd{public static void main(String args[]){Thrd1 th1 = new Thrd1();th1.start();Thrd2 th2 = new Thrd2();th2.start();Thrd3 th3 = new Thrd3();th3.start();![](images/1.png)} } |
| Output:![](images/Figure_6.5_(1).png) |

Table 6.1: Example of thread class

After the discussion on thread creating, we immediately shift our focus to the thread priorities and how they are implemented.

[Comparison between Runnable interface and thread class](#contents.xhtml_sc3_181a)

In this section, we are going to present the comparison between runnable interface and thread class in a pointwise manner:

*   Multiple inheritance in not supported in Java: We can extend another class when we implement the Runnable interface, but we cannot extend another class when we extend the Thread class since Java does not allow multiple inheritance. So, implementing Runnable and extending Thread do the same thing, but implementing Runnable leaves us with the option of extending another class. As a result, it is preferable to use Runnable interface.
*   Safety of thread: When we implement the Runnable interface, the same object is shared by several threads, but when we extend the Thread class, each thread is assigned a separate object.
*   Property of inheritance: When we extend Thread, all of the Thread classʼs features are inherited, but when we implement the Runnable interface, no further features are inherited because Runnable just has one abstract function, the run() method. As a result, implementing Runnable interface means performing lightweight operation.
*   Use of interface: Even Java recommends coding to interface. So, we must implement Runnable rather than extending thread. Also, Thread class implements Runnable interface.
*   Do not extend unless you modify fundamental behavior of class: Only when you want to change run() method and other methods, extend the Thread class. If you just want to change the run() method, implementing the Runnable interface is the best choice having only one abstract method (run()). We must not extend the Thread class unless we want to change its core behavior.
*   Flexibility in code when we implement Runnable: When we extend Thread first, all thread characteristics are inherited, and our class becomes a direct subclass of Thread, so any action we perform is in the Thread class. However, when we implement Runnable, we establish a new thread and send the runnable object as a parameter, we can pass the runnable object to executorService, and we can do a lot more. So, when we use Runnable, we have more possibilities which leads to code flexibility.

[Thread priority](#contents.xhtml_sc2_182a)

Each thread is given a priority by Java, which specifies how it should be handled in relation to the other threads. The relative priority of one thread to another is indicated by thread priorities, which are integers.

To determine when to move from one running thread to the next, a threadʼs priority is employed instead. A context switch would be appropriate here. The following guidelines for determining whether a context switch occurs are straightforward:

*   A thread can voluntarily relinquish control. This is done by explicitly yielding, sleeping, or blocking on pending I/O. In this scenario, all other threads are examined, and the highest-priority thread that is ready to run is given the CPU.
*   A thread can be preempted by a higher-priority thread. In this case, a lower-priority thread that does not yield the processor is simply preempted—no matter what it is doing— by a higher-priority thread. Basically, as soon as a higher-priority thread wants to run, it does. This is called preemptive multitasking.

In cases where two threads with the same priority are competing for CPU cycles, the situation is a bit complicated. For operating systems such as Windows, threads of equal priority are time-sliced automatically in round-robin fashion. For other types of operating systems, threads of equal priority must voluntarily yield control to their peers. If they do not, the other threads will not run.

[Setting thread priorities](#contents.xhtml_sc3_183a)

Thread priorities are used by the thread scheduler to decide when each thread should be allowed to run. Higher priority threads get more CPU time than lower priority threads. Rank of values of priority for a thread is in one to ten. There are three static variables defined in Thread class for priority, mentioned as follows:

*   public static int MIN_PRIORITY: This is minimum priority that a thread can have. Value for this is one.
*   public static int NORM_PRIORITY: This is default priority of a thread if do not explicitly define it. Value for this is five.
*   public static int MAX_PRIORITY: This is maximum priority of a thread. Value for this is ten.

Java permits to set the priority of a thread using setPriority()  method which is a member of Thread.

For example,  ThreadName.setPriority(intNumber); /* intNumber is an integer value to which the threadʼs priority is set.*/

So, you can write ThreadName.setPriority(5);

You can also write the following:

*   ThreadName.setPriority(Thread.MIN_PRIORITY);// priority is set to 1, or
*   ThreadName.setPriority(Thread.NORM_PRIORITY);//  priority is set to 5
*   ThreadName.setPriority(Thread.MAX_PRIORITY);//  priority is set to 10

You can also obtain the current priority using getPriority() method which is a method of Thread. For example,  System.out.println(th1.getPriority());

In [Table 6.2](#ch06.xhtml_tab6-2), the concept of implementing priority is given:

| import java.lang.Thread; |
| class Thrd1 extends Thread{public void run(){for(inti = 1; i<=5; i++){System.out.println("Thread1" + " "+i);}}} | class Thrd2 extends Thread{public void run(){for(int j = 1; j <=5; j++){System.out.println("Thread2" + " "+j);}}} | class Thrd3 extends Thread{public void run(){for(int k = 1; k <=5; k++){System.out.println("Thread3" + " "+k);}}} |
| public class TstThrd2{public static void main(String args[]){Thrd1 th1 = new Thrd1();Thrd2 th2 = new Thrd2();Thrd3 th3 = new Thrd3();th1.setPriority(1);th2.setPriority(10);th3.setPriority(5);th1.start();th2.start();th3.start();}} |
| Output:![](images/Figure_6.5_(1)1.png) |

Table 6.2: Example of thread priority

Now, the main focus is on identifying the main and child thread and their priorities.

[Main and child thread using priority](#contents.xhtml_sc4_184a)

In this section, we are going to identify thread using the main thread and child thread and also explore the concept of getPriority() and setPriority() to get and set priorities of threads using programming examples. Now, let us take a look at the definition of the previously mentioned methods:

*   Thread.currentThread().getPriority(): It will return the priority of main thread in the code.
*   Thread.currentThread().setPriority(Thread.MAX_PRIORITY): It will set the priority of main thread in the code.

In [Table 6.3](#ch06.xhtml_tab6-3), we present the concept with a small java program:

| import java.lang.Thread; |
| class Thrd1 extends Thread{public void run(){for(inti = 1; i<=5; i++){System.out.println("child thread is running");}}} | public class TstThrd4{ public static void main(String args[]){Thrd1 th1 = new Thrd1();System.out.println("Priority of main thread is "+Thread.currentThread().getPriority());System.out.println("Priority of child thread is "+th1.getPriority());Thread.currentThread().setPriority(Thread.MAX_PRIORITY);System.out.println("Priority of child thread is set to ");th1.setPriority(7);th1.start();System.out.println(th1.getPriority());for(inti = 1; i<=5; i++){System.out.println("main thread is running");}} } |
| Output:![](images/Figure_6.6_(1).png) |

Table 6.3: Example of main and child thread priority

In the next section, the benefits of multi-threading are elaborated.

[Benefits of multi-threading](#contents.xhtml_sc2_185a)

One of multithreadingʼs key advantages is the concurrent activity that speeds up programs. Multi-threading enables the efficient usage of several processing subsystems as well as the full exploitation of parallel hardware. While multi-threading is crucial for maximizing the performance of symmetric multiprocessors, it also boosts performance on systems with a single processor by increasing the overlap of processes like computation and I/O. Some of the most important benefits of multi-threading are:

*   Improved throughput: Many concurrent computer operations and I/O requests within a single process.
*   Simultaneous and fully symmetric use of multiple processors for computation and I/O
*   Superior application responsiveness: If a request can be launched on its own thread, applications do not freeze or show the hourglass. An entire application will not block, or otherwise wait, pending the completion of another request.
*   Improved server responsiveness: Large or complex requests or slow clients do not block other requests for service. The overall throughput of the server is much greater.
*   Minimized system resource usage: Threads impose minimal impact on system resources. Threads require less overhead to create, maintain, and manage than a traditional process.
*   Program structure simplification: Threads can be used to simplify the structure of complex applications, such as server-class and multimedia applications. Simple routines can be written for each activity, making complex programs easier to design and code, and more adaptive to a wide variation in user demands.
*   Better communication: Thread synchronization functions can be used to provide enhanced process-to-process communication. In addition, sharing large amounts of data through separate threads of execution within the same address space provides extremely high-bandwidth, low-latency communication between separate tasks within an application.

[Important methods of thread programming](#contents.xhtml_sc2_186a)

With the extended discussion, methods are frequently used for thread programming in Java. Some of them are mentioned in the following section with respect to programming aspects.

[Description of methods](#contents.xhtml_sc3_187a)

We have provided a simple but important list of method definitions, that will help application program development:

*   void start(): It is used to start the execution of the thread.
*   void run(): It is used to do an action for a thread.
*   int getPriority():  It returns the priority of the thread.
*   void setPriority():  It changes the priority of the thread.
*   void suspend(): It is used to suspend the thread.
*   void resume(): It is used to resume the suspended thread.
*   void stop():  It is used to stop the thread.
*   void notify():  It is used to give the notification for only one thread which is waiting for a particular object.
*   void notifyAll(): It is used to give the notification to all waiting threads of a particular object.

The following are the two most important methods join() and isAlive(), which are depicted with examples.

[join() and isAlive() method in thread](#contents.xhtml_sc3_188a)

There are two ways to determine whether a thread has finished. First, you can call isAlive()  on the thread. This method is defined by Thread, and its general form is shown as follows:

final Boolean isAlive( )

The isAlive() method returns true if the thread upon which it is called is still running. It returns false otherwise. While isAlive() is occasionally useful, the method that you will more commonly use to wait for a thread to finish is called join(), shown as follows:

final void join( ) throws InterruptedException

This method waits until the thread on which it is called terminates. Its name comes from the concept of the calling thread waiting until the specified thread joins it. Additional forms of join() allow you to specify a maximum amount of time that you want to wait for the specified thread to terminate. In [Table 6.4](#ch06.xhtml_tab6-4), the examples are provided:

| import java.lang.Thread;class Thrd1 extends Thread{public void run(){for(inti = 1; i<=5; i++){System.out.println("Thread1" + " i is "+i);}}} | class Thrd2 extends Thread{public void run(){for(int j = 1; j <=5; j++){System.out.println("Thread2" + " j is "+j);}}} |
| public class TstThrd6{ public static void main(String args[]){Thrd1 th1 = new Thrd1();Thrd2 th2 = new Thrd2();th1.start();th2.start();System.out.println(th1.isAlive()); /* it will return true*/System.out.println(th2.isAlive());try{th1.join(); }catch(InterruptedExceptionie){} }} |
| Output:![](images/Figure_6.7_(1).png) |

Table 6.4: Example of join() and isAlive() in Thread

Now, we will give four examples of thread programming in terms of Thread.sleep() method, renaming a thread and performing a single task by multiple threads.

[Method sleep() with positive timeout value](#contents.xhtml_sc3_189a)

Thread.sleep() causes the current thread to suspend execution for a specified period. This is an efficient means of making processor time available to the other threads of an application or other applications that might be running on a computer system.

Now, we are going to present the source code for the sleep() with positive timeout value:

class TstThrd10 extends Thread{

public void run(){

for(int i=1;i<5;i++){

try{

if (i==3)

{

System.out.println("The thread is sleeping when the value of i is 3");

System.out.println("...........");

Thread.sleep(1500);

continue;

}

}catch(InterruptedException e){System.out.println(e);}

System.out.println("The value of i is "+ i);

}

}

public static void main(String args[]){

TstThrd10 t1=new TstThrd10();

TstThrd10 t2=new TstThrd10();

t1.start();

t2.start();

}

}

The output of the code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_6.8_(1).png)

Figure 6.4: sleep() with positive timeout value

In the preceding example, Thread.sleep(1500) method is used to suspend execution for a specified period that is 1500 milliseconds. To depict the situation, we use the statement System.out.println("..........."); when the value of the variable becomes 3, the thread will be suspended for 1500 milliseconds after printing the ........... in the output.

[Method sleep() with negative timeout value](#contents.xhtml_sc3_190a)

The value of the argument for Thread.sleep() takes milliseconds but cannot be negative, otherwise it will lead to an IllegalArgumentException. The example is as follows:

class TstThrd9 extends Thread{

public void run(){

for(int i=1;i<5;i++){

try{

if (i==3)

{

System.out.println("The thread is sleeping when the value of i is 3");

Thread.sleep(-500);

continue;

}

}catch(InterruptedException e){System.out.println(e);}

System.out.println("The value of i is "+ i);

}

}

public static void main(String args[]){

TstThrd9 t1=new TstThrd9();

TstThrd9 t2=new TstThrd9();

t1.start();

t2.start();

}

}

The output of the preceding program is as follows:

![](images/Figure_6.9.png)

Figure 6.5: caption sleep() with negative timeout value

In the preceding coding example, the exception is generated when the value of the variable i is 3. This appends due to the negative value -500 of the argument for Thread.sleep() method leading to the IllegalArgumentException. So, the argument value of Thread.sleep() method must not be negative in the programming.

[Program for naming a thread](#contents.xhtml_sc3_191a)

The methods getName() and setName() is used to get and set the names of the threads created. The following program demonstrates the use of these two methods:

class TstThrd7 extends Thread

{

public void run(){

System.out.println("The thread is running...");

}

public static void main(String args[]){

TstThrd7 t1=new TstThrd7();

TstThrd7 t2=new TstThrd7();

System.out.println("Name of thread1:"+t1.getName());

System.out.println("Name of thread2:"+t2.getName());

t1.start();

t2.start();

t1.setName("Subhajit Adhikari");

System.out. println("After changing name of thread1:"+t1.getName());

}

}

The output of the preceding program is as follows:

![](images/Figure_6.10.png)

Figure 6.6: How to give a name to a thread

In the preceding program, there are two thread objects are created as t1 and t2. Then t1.getName() and t2.getName() methods are used to display the names of two thread as Thread-0 and Thread-1. Now, we can set the names using the statement t1.setName("Subhajit Adhikari");, then if you use t1.getName() again in a System.out.println("After changing name of thread1:"+t1.getName()); statement the change of name is reflected in the output.

[Program for single task by multiple threads](#contents.xhtml_sc3_192a)

In the following programming example, one simple task is doing by multiple threads:

class TstThred8 extends Thread{

public void run(){

System.out.println("Same task is doing");

}

public static void main(String args[]){

TstThred8 t1=new TstThred8();

TstThred8 t2=new TstThred8();

TstThred8 t3=new TstThred8();

t2.start();

t3.start();

The output of the preceding program is as follows:

![](images/Figure_6.11.png)

Figure 6.7: One simple task is doing by multiple threads

In the preceding example, there are three threads t1, t2 and t3 are performing same task. With the help of thread class, we have created three different thread objects. Then using start() method, the working of each individual threads are called. Thus, the output.

[Thread synchronization](#contents.xhtml_sc2_193a)

It is already mentioned that threads in Java are running in the same memory space, and hence it is easy to communicate between two threads. Inter-thread communications allow threads to talk to or wait on each other. Again, because all the threads in a program share the same memory space, it is possible for two threads to access the same variables and methods in object. Problems may occur when two or more threads are accessing the same data concurrently, for example, one thread stores data into the shared object and the other threads reads data, there can be synchronization problem if the first thread has not finished storing the data before the second one goes to read it. So we need to take care to access the data by only one thread process at a time. Java provides unique language level support for such synchronization. In this section, we will learn how synchronization mechanism and inter-thread communications are possible in Java.

To solve the critical section problem, one usual concept is known as monitor. A monitor is an object which is used as a mutually exclusive lock, called mutex. Only one thread may own a monitor at a given time. When a thread acquires a lock, it is said to have entered the monitor. All other threads attempting to enter the locked monitor will be suspended until the owner thread exits the monitor. However, in Java, there is no such monitor. In fact, all Java object have their own implicit monitor associated with them. Here, the key word synchronized is used by which method(s) or block of statements can be made protected from the simultaneous access. With this, when a class is designed with threads in mind, the class designer decides which methods should not be allowed to execute concurrently. When a class with synchronized method is instantiated, the new object is given its own implicit monitor. The entire time that a thread is inside of a synchronized method, all other threads that try to call any other synchronized method on the same instance have to wait. In order to exit the monitor and relinquish control of the object to the next waiting thread, the monitor owner simply needs to return from the method.

Suppose we want to maintain a bank account of customers. Several transactions, such as depositing some amount to an account and withdraw some amount from an account etc., are possible. Now, for a given account, if two or more transactions come simultaneously then only one transaction should be allowed at a time instead of simultaneous transaction processing so that data inconsistency will never occur. So, what we need is to synchronize the transaction.

For example: The following Java application shows how the transactions in a bank can be carried out concurrently:

class AccDetail {

public int Abalance;

public int accNo;

void disp Balance() {

System.out.println(" The Acc No is " + accNo + " and The Balance is " + Abalance);

}

synchronized void deposit(int amt){

Abalance = Abalance + amt;

System.out.println( " The amount "+ amt + " deposited");

dispBalance();

}

synchronized void withdraw(int amt){

Abalance = Abalance - amt;

System.out.println( " The amount "+ amt + " is withdrawn");

dispBalance();

}

}

class TransDeposit implements Runnable{

int amt;

AccDetail accountX;

TransDeposit(AccDetail x, int amt){

accountX = x;

this.amt = amt;

new Thread(this).start();

}

public void run(){

accountX.deposit(amt);

}

}

class TransWithdraw implements Runnable{

int amt;

AccDetail accountY;

TransWithdraw(AccDetail y, int amt) {

accountY = y;

this.amt = amt;

new Thread(this).start();

}

public void run(){

accountY.withdraw(amt);

}

}

class TransactionDemo{

public static void main(String args[]) {

AccDetail objAcc = new AccDetail();

objAcc.Abalance = 5000;

objAcc.accNo = 4568;

System.out.println(" The account number is "+ objAcc.accNo );

System.out.println(" The initial balance is "+ objAcc.Abalance );

TransDeposit t1;

TransWithdraw t2;

t1 = new TransDeposit(objAcc, 500);

t2 = new TransWithdraw(objAcc,900);

}

}

The output of the preceding program is as follows:

![](images/Figure_6.12.png)

Figure 6.8: Java application shows how the transactions occur in a bank

In the preceding example, in the class AccDetail, the keyword synchronized is used for the methods void deposit() and void withdraw() so that these two methods will never run for the same object instance simultaneously. The two classes as TransDeposit and TransWithdraw implements Runnable interface and the method void run() is used twice to invoke methods as deposit() and withdraw() to perform two task of depositing and withdrawing amount from the same account in a synchronized manner.

[Inter-thread communication](#contents.xhtml_sc2_194a)

There are three ways for threads to communicate with each other. The first is through commonly shared data. All the threads in the same program share the same memory space. If an object is accessible to various threads, then these threads share access to that objectʼs data member and thus communicate each other.

The second way for threads to communicate is by using thread control methods. There are such three such methods by which threads communicate with each other: suspend(), resume(), join(). These methods can be used for the caller thread to wait for the completion of called thread.

The third way for threads to communicate is the use of three methods; wait(), notify(), and notifyAll(); these are defined in class Object of package java.lang. Actually, these three methods provide an elegant inter-process communication mechanism to take care of the deadlock situation in Java. As there is multi-threading in program, deadlock may occur when a thread holding the key to monitor is suspended or waiting for another threadʼs completion. If the other thread is waiting for needs to get into the same monitor, both threads will be waiting for ever. The uses of these three methods are briefed as follows:

*   wait( ): This method tells the calling thread to give up the monitor and make the calling thread wait until either a time out occurs or another thread calls the same threadʼs notify() or notifyAll() method.
*   Notify( ): This method wakes up the only one (first) waiting thread called wait() on the same object.
*   notifyAll( ): This method will wake up all the threads that have been called wait() on the same object

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_195a)

Different real-life applications are actually based on multi-threading programming aspect. In this chapter, the concept of thread programming is outlined. The learner will get better understanding of threads which is very useful for working in shared environment and low overhead. The building blocks of thread programs are discussed in simpler examples but programmatically. Another concept of thread synchronization is depicted in this chapter with a goal to portray what actually the concept is and how it is incorporated in Java programming. We have also included a small application of banking transaction to demonstrate this concept. Lastly, the whole discussion is wrapped up with the inter thread commination and how it can be implemented in Java using threads.

In the next chapter, the concept of exception and how it is handled in Java programming is illustrated with examples.
