# [CHAPTER 7Exception Handling in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch07a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_200a)

Every day, everyone faces some unexpected events, in programming, this is known as an exception. When an exception has occurred, the most important thing is to describe the necessary information about the exception. Next, we have to overcome from the exceptional event, in programming, it is known as exception handling. In this chapter, if a program code generates one exception, the way to handle it is described in detail. In this regard, the term error is defined first and then how it can lead to generate exception is presented. The different types of errors are discussed. Then, exception is defined and how exception handling can be done in Java is elaborated using programs.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_201a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Error
*   Exception
*   Exception handling using try-catch-finally block
*   Advantages of exception
*   Grouping and differentiating error types
*   Exception handling and method overriding

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_202a)

The primary goal of this chapter is to build the fundamental knowledge of error and exception. To do that, first, the error types are presented with several examples for better understanding of learners. This chapter deals with the differences between Error and Exception, which is very important for learners to begin with. Then, the concept of Exception is presented with real life example. Next, the chapter gives idea about different types of Exceptions can be possible in Java. The most important contribution of this chapter is to define Exception Handling in such a way that everyone can extract the meaning of it and can remember the concept easily. The basic building blocks of handling of different exceptions are the try block and catch block along with finally statement and their usage are also presented in a well-defined manner in this chapter. Now, the discussion begins with the definition of Errors in the next paragraph.

[Error](#contents.xhtml_sc2_203a)

Errors are the wrongs that can make a program go wrong. An error may produce an incorrect output or may terminate the execution of the program or even may cause the system to crash. Errors are classified into two categories, as mentioned in the next sections.

[Compile time error](#contents.xhtml_sc3_204a)

All syntax errors will be detected and displayed by the Java compiler. These errors are known as compile-time errors. Whenever the compiler displays an error, it will not create the .class file. So, it is necessary that we fix all the errors before we can successfully compile and run the program. Most of the compile time errors are due to spelling mistakes. The most common problems are as follows:

*   Errors regarding syntax: The different types are explained as follows:
    *   Error of “… expected”: The syntax of Java is very specific about the required punctuation. This error occurs when you forgot to put a semicolon at the end of a statement or put balance parentheses, as shown in the following snippet:

        class TstErr1{

        public static void main(String s[]){

        System.out.println("Hello") //// Error, missing semicolon

        }

        }

    *   Error of “unclosed string literal”: String literals must be enclosed in quotation marks. This error occurs if you fail to terminate the literal with quotation marks, shown as follow:

        class TstErr3{

        public static void main(String s[]){

        String st="Hii;

        System.out.println(st);

        }

        }

    *   Error of “illegal start of expression”: This error occurs when an expression is expected but not found:
        *   Putting an extra right parenthesis, after if condition, shown as follows:

            if (a > b) ) // Error, extra parenthesis

            *   Not giving the right-hand side of an assignment statement, shown as follows:

                mx = ; // Error, missing right-hand side

    *   Error of being “not a statement”: The specific type of error can be pointed out at the time of a syntactically correct statement does not appear in the required place, as shown in the following examples:
        *   Error of “Writing an assignment statement without the assignment operator”:

            a ; // Error, missing =

        *   Error of “Misspelling else:”:

            if (a > b)

            temp = a;

            else ; // Error, else not spelled correctly

You do not receive else expected, since you do not need to create an else alternative, therefore this only seems to be a not a good statement.

*   Errors regarding identifier:  The different types are explained as follows:
    *   Error of “cannot find symbol”: This is most likely the most prevalent type of mistake found at the time of compilation. All identifiers in Java must be defined before they can be used, and an inconsistency between the declaration and usage of an identifier will result in this error. Check the spelling of the identification carefully. It is simple to make a mistake by writing a lower-case letter instead of an upper-case character, or to mix the letters O and l with the numbers 0 and 1:
        *   Error of “. . . is already defined in . . .”: An identifier can only be declared once in the same scope, shown as follows:

            int sum = 0;

            double sum = 0.0; // Error, sum already defined

        *   Error of “array required but . . . found”: This error is caused by attempting to index a variable which is not an array, shown as follows:

            int max(int a, int b) {

            if (a > b) return a;

            else return b[i]; // Error, b is not an array

            }

*   Errors regarding computation: These types of errors results are generated from code that is syntactically correct but violates the semantics of Java programming language, to be very specific terms of type checking rules, mentioned as follows:
    *   Error of “variable . . . might not have been initialized”: A default initial value is assigned to an instance variable specified in a class. A local variable created within a method, on the other hand, does not, therefore you must initialize it before using it, either in its declaration or in an assignment statement, shown as follows:

        void disp(int v) {

        int d, e;

        d = 2;

        int h = 1;

        if(d == v) // Correct

        h = e; // Found an Error, e is not initialized

        else

        t = h;

        }

    *   Error of “. . . in . . . cannot be applied to . . .”: This type of error message is quite common and is caused by an inconsistency between a call of a method and declaration of the particular method, shown as follows:

        void test(int k) { ... }

        m(15.15); // Error, the literal is of type double, not int

    *   Error of operator . . . cannot be applied to . . . ,. . : Although implicit type conversion is permitted between certain numeric types, operators are only provided for specific kinds, shown as follows:

        int tm = 5;

        boolean tn = true;

        int ts = tm + tn; // Found an Error, can’t add a boolean value

        double tm= tm + 1.4; // Correct, int is implicitly converted to double

    *   Error of “possible loss of precision”: This error occurs when attempting to assign a higher precision value to a lower precision variable without using an explicit type casting operation. Interestingly, literals of type floating-point are of type double, and attempting to assign one to a variable of type float will result in this warning, shown as follows:

        float sm = 0.0; // Found an Error, literal is not of type float

        The correct way to do this is to use a literal of type float or an explicit type cast, shown as follows:

        float fsum = 0.0f;

        float fsum = (float) 0.0;

    *   Error of “incompatible types”: Java checks that the type of an expression is compatible with the type of the variable used in coding statement with assignment operation and an error will occur if they are incompatible, shown as follows:

        Boolean bl = true;

        int at = bt; // Found an Error, can’t assign boolean to int

    *   Error of “inconvertible types”: In this type of error, not all type conversion is legal. The example is as follows:

        boolean bt = true;

        int tx = (int) bt; // Found an Error, can’t convert boolean to int

*   Errors regarding return statements: These types of errors are linked to the structure of methods and their return statements; in general, they are simple to repair, mentioned as follows:
    *   Error of “Missing return statement”: When a method returns a non-void type, every path that leaves the method must have a return-statement, despite the fact that there is no way that the path can be executed, shown as follows:

        int max(int it, int jt) {

        if (it> jt) return it;

        else if (it<= jt) return jt;

        // Found an Error: what about the path when i>j and i<=j are both false?!!

        }

    *   Error of “Missing return value”: A method returning a type must have a return-statement that includes an expression of the correct type, shown as follows:

        int fmax(int at, int bt) {

        return; // Found an Error, missing int expression

        }

    *   Error of “cannot return a value from method whose result type is void”: A statement of return within a void method, on the other hand, must not include an expression, as shown in the following command:

        void Ftest(int ta, int tb) {

        return ta + tb; // Found an Error, the method was declared void

        }

    *   Error of “invalid method declaration; return type required”: Each and every method except constructors must have a return type or void specified; if not, this error will arise, shown as follows:

        fmax(int i, int j) {

        ...

        }

    *   Error of “unreachable statement”: If you write a statement after a return statement, the following error will occur:

        void test(int Tj) {

        System.out.println("Value is " + Tj);

        return;

        Tj++;

        }

*   Errors regarding access to static entities: The modifier static means that a variable or method is associated with a class and not with individual objects of a class. Normally, static entities are rarely used in Java (except for the declaration of constants), the reason is that the programs which are written as classes, must be instantiated to create at least one object.
    *   Error of “non-static variable . . . cannot be referenced from a static context”: As the method main is (needed to be) static, so must every variable specified in the class that is referenced by the method. An example without specifying the modifier results in a compile-time error, is shown as follows:

        class MyTest {

        int tm;

        public static void main(String[] args) {

        System.out.println(tm); // Found an Error

        }

        }

    *   Error of “non-static method . . . cannot be referenced from a static context”: In a comparable manner, a method (of non-static in nature) cannot be called from a static method such as main; the rationale is a little more nuanced. It is pointless to invoke a method (that is non-static in nature) from a static method in the without the presence of an object, shown as follows:

        class MyTest {

        void m(int n) {…}

        public static void main(String[] args) {

        m(50);

        }

        }

After the detailed discussion of the different types of compile-time errors, it is the perfect time to present the different run time errors can be possible in Java programming language.

[Run time error](#contents.xhtml_sc3_205a)

At the time of interpretation, Java detects a runtime error. Then, it throws an exception and outputs a trace of the stack that shows the whole call stack as the sequence of methods called from the main program until the line that produced the exception. The following code is trying to divide a number by zero. If you execute the code in [Figure 7.1](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-1), Java will throw exception.

![](images/Figure_7.1.png)

Figure 7.1: Example of runtime error

The output of the preceding code is given as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.2.png)

Figure 7.2: Example of an exception

From the preceding output, we can have detailed information about the exception, like class name (TstExp), method name (main), file name (TstExp.java), line number (8) and the cause of exception(/ by zero).

The following are the different types of errors:

*   Errors regarding out of range: The majority of these exceptions occur when attempting to access an array or string with an index outside its boundaries, mentioned as follows:
    *   Error of ArrayIndexOutOfRange: This exception is issued when attempting to index an array a[i] when the index i is not inside the values 0, a.length, included, as shown in the following command:

        int arr[] = { 10, 20, 30, 40, 50 };

        for (inti = 0; i<= arr.length; i++)

        System.out.println(arr[i]);

    *   Error of StringIndexOutOfRange: This exception is thrown by many methods of the class String when a value of index is not within the range of values 0 and s.length(), inclusive, shown as follows

        Note: s.length() is valid and means the position just after the last character in the string.

        String str = "JAVAPROGRAM";

        System.out.println(str.charAt(30));

    *   Dereferencing null: When you declare a variable of a reference type, you only receive a variable that can store a reference to a class object. At this point, trying to access a field or method of the class will cause the exception NullPointerException to be raised.
*   Errors regarding computation: The next three exceptions occur when you try to convert a string to a number and the form of the string does not match that of a number, for example 12a3:
    *   Error of InputMismatchException: This exception is thrown by the class Scanner, which is a class introduced into version 5 of Java programming language to make character-oriented input simpler to programs.
    *   Error of IllegalFormatException: This exception is raised by the format function in the String class, which allows for output using format specifiers similar to those in C.
    *   Error of NumberFormatException: This exception is thrown by methods declared in the numeric wrapper classes like Integer and Double, specifically by the methods parseInt and parseDouble that convert from strings to the primitive numeric types.
    *   Error of ArithmeticException: When you try to divide by zero, this type of exception is raised.
*   Error regarding insufficient memory: Modern computers have very large memories, so you are unlikely to encounter in case of routine programming. Though, they can occur as a side effect of other mistakes. Some of them are:
    *   Errors of outOfMemoryError: It can be thrown if you run out of memory:
    *   Error of StackOverFlowArea: A stack is used to store the activation record of each method that is called; it contains the parameters and the local variables as well as other information such as the return address. The unbounded recursion can lead to the Java Virtual Machine running out of stack space.
*   Error regarding program execution: To interpret a Java program from within an environment or by executing the interpreter from the command line, you have to write: java Test, where Test is the name of a class containing a main method.

    Note: It is helpful to define main methods in the majority of classes and utilize them to test each class independently. After the classes are combined into a single program, you simply need to guarantee that the interpreter is called on the class with the real main method. If the interpreter fails to discover and run the program, two runtime errors may result.

    *   Error of NoClassDefFoundError: The interpreter must be able to locate the file containing a class with a main method, such as Test.class. If packages are not utilized, they must be placed in the directory where the interpreter is executed. Check that the name of the class matches the name of the file without the extension. The case is crucial. First, you compile the program: javac MyClass.java and then you recall that line and erase the c from javac to obtain the command for the interpreter, shown as follows:

        java MyClass.java

        However, MyClass.java is not the name of the class, thus you will receive this exception. To use the interpreter, you must also remove the.java extension, shown as follows:

        java MyClass

    *   Error of NoSuchMethodFoundError: main: This error will occur if there is no method main in the class, or if the declaration is not precisely correct: the static modifier, the return type void, the method name main written in lower case, and one parameter of type String[]. In the situation, when you forget the public  modifier, this message Main method not public will be coming.

Let us elaborate on the concept of Exception, Exception Hierarchy, Exception types, Exception Handling operations and examples of try-catch-finally blocks one after another.

[Exception](#contents.xhtml_sc2_206a)

The term exception is an abbreviation for exceptional event. An exception is a scenario during program execution that breaks the regular flow of instructions. When a method encounters an error, the method produces an object and passes it to the runtime system. The object, known as an exception object, includes information about the error, such as its kind and the programʼs state at the time the error occurred. Throwing an exception is the act of creating an exception object and passing it to the runtime system. The Java runtime system tries to find a way to handle it when a method throws an exception. The set of available somethings to handle the exception is the ordered list of methods that were called to get to the method where the mistake occurred. The call stack is a list of methods.

![](images/Figure_7.3.png)

Figure 7.3: Method call hierarchy

The runtime system scans the call stack for a method that contains an exception-handling piece of code. This section of code is known as an exception handler. The search process starts with the method where the error occurred and progresses along the call stack in the opposite order in which the methods were called, as shown in [Figure 7.3](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-3). When an acceptable handler is discovered, the exception is sent to the handler by the runtime system. [Figure 7.4](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-4) depicts an acceptable exception handler at the time of the type of the thrown exception object matches the type that may be handled by the handler. The selected exception handler is said to catch the exception. If the runtime system thoroughly scans all the methods on the call stack and fails to find out an exception handler appropriate for it, as shown in the next figure, then the runtime system (and, consequently, the program) also terminates.

![](images/Figure_7.4.png)

Figure 7.4: Method call hierarchy with exception handling

[Exception hierarchy](#contents.xhtml_sc3_207a)

The class Exception and its subclasses are a type of Throwable that specifies conditions that a reasonable application would want to catch. The non-subclasses of RuntimeException class and Exception class are considered as checked exceptions.

The type of checked exceptions must be specified in the method or constructorʼs throws statement, whether they can be thrown with the help of the method or constructorʼs execution and spread outside the boundary of the method or constructor. The partial exception hierarchy is given in the following [Figure 7.5](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-5):

![](images/Figure_7.5.png)

Figure 7.5: Exception hierarchy

The most generic exception type, java.lang.Exception, is found in the java.lang package, while the majority of its subclasses are found in other packages. Some IOException classes are found in the java.io package, whereas others are found in the java.net package.

[Built-in exceptions](#contents.xhtml_sc3_208a)

There are two types of built-in exceptions are possible in Java. They are discussed below.

[Checked exceptions](#contents.xhtml_sc4_209a)

The types of all exceptions other than Runtime Exceptions are known as checked exceptions. As the compiler checks at the time of compilation to find out, if the programmer has taken necessary mechanisms to handle them or not. The compilation error will occur, if these exceptions are not handled/declared in the program. There are some of the checked exceptions like ClassNotFoundException, IOException, EOFException, FileNotFoundException, NoSuchMethodException present in Java. In [Figure 7.6](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-6), the example is demonstrated:

![](images/Figure_7.6.png)

Figure 7.6: Example of checked exception

The output of the preceding code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.7.png)

Figure 7.7: Output of checked exception

In the preceding example, we can see that the FileNotFoundException has occurred at compile time, because there is no such file abc2.txt in the specified location and this is a checked exception.

[Unchecked exceptions](#contents.xhtml_sc4_210a)

A type of runtime exceptions is also known as unchecked exceptions. They are not checked at the time of compilation, leaving them as the responsibility of the programmer to take necessary steps to handle such exceptions, and make a safe exit. Some of the unchecked exceptions are listed as follows:

*   ArithmeticException
*   IllegalArgumentException
*   NumberFormatException
*   IndexOutOfBoundsException
*   ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException
*   StringIndexOutOfBoundsException
*   NullPointerException

In the following [Figure 7.8](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-8), there is one source code presenting the unchecked exception:

![](images/Figure_7.8.png)

Figure 7.8: Example of unchecked exception

In the preceding example in [Figure 7.8](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-8), there is one ArithmeticException has occurred due to divide by zero error, but the program compiles fine, in runtime it will generate the exception. That is why it is known as unchecked exception. Unchecked exceptions, in most situations, represent unrecoverable programming logic mistakes. A NullPointerException is triggered, for example, if you try to access an object with a reference variable not assigning an object to it. The type of exception like IndexOutOfBoundsException is thrown, at the time when you access an element of an array element not in the arrayʼs limits. These are the logic flaws that must be addressed in the software. Unchecked exceptions can arise at any time throughout the programʼs execution. Java does not make it mandatory for you to create code to catch unchecked exceptions in order to avoid the clumsy misuse of try and catch code blocks. In the next section, the differences between checked and unchecked exceptions are tabulated.

[Difference between checked and unchecked exception](#contents.xhtml_sc4_211a)

In the following [Table 7.1](#ch07.xhtml_tab7-1), the checked and unchecked exceptions are differentiated according to the nature of the exception and how they are handled:

| Checked exception | Unchecked exception |
| --- | --- |
| The duty of the compiler to handle this type of Exceptions. | The task of compiler is not to check this type of exceptions. |
| These are extended from java.lang.Exception  class | These are extended from java.lang.RuntimeException class |
| These are handled by the code itself with the help of coding block of try and catch or throws keyword | These are handled by the JVM |
| The program doesnʼt compile if there is an unhandled checked exception in the program code. | The program compiles successfully even if there is an unhandled unchecked exception in the program code. |

Table 7.1: Difference between checked and unchecked exception

In accordance with this context, the most important concept of user defined exceptions are discussed.

[User-defined exceptions](#contents.xhtml_sc3_212a)

Java allows users to write their own exception classes that are derived from the built-in class Exception. The Exception class inherits all of the methods from the Throwable class. A number of in-built exceptions are included in Java to handle common error conditions, but predefined exceptions, on the other hand, may fail to capture an applicationʼs particular requirements. In some cases, Java allows programmers to design their own unique exceptions, termed as User-Defined Exceptions or also known as custom exceptions. Developers may improve code readability, improve error reporting, segregate business logic from exception handling, and handle unusual scenarios by designing custom exceptions. In the following [Figure 7.9](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-9), the concept of user defined exception is given in terms of Java code:

![](images/Figure_7.9.png)

Figure 7.9: Example of user defined exception

The output of the preceding code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.10.png)

Figure 7.10: Output of user defined exception

Before going to discuss the different operations related to exception handling, it is very important to learn the basic differences between error and exception, discussed in the next section.

[Difference between error and exception](#contents.xhtml_sc3_213a)

The differences between the error and exception are tabulated in the following [Table 7.2](#ch07.xhtml_tab7-2):

| Error | Exception |
| Errors specifies that something having huge impact occurred. | Exceptions are events that occur in the code |
| Errors are unchecked | Exception is checked and unchecked. |
| The error cannot be recoveredby the programmer. | A programmer can control these scenarios with the help of actions that are corrective and necessary also. |

Table 7.2: Difference between error and exception in Java

In the next section, we will discuss about the exception handling operations in details.

[Operations regarding exception handling](#contents.xhtml_sc3_214a)

There are five keywords related to exception handling and they are try, catch, finally, throws and throw. To process an exception when it occurs, the line that throws the exception is executed within a try block. A try block is followed by one or more catch clauses, which contain code to process an exception

Each catch clause has an associated exception type. When an exception occurs, processing continues at the first catch clause that matches the exception type. A finally block is where you put code that must run regardless of an exception. The coding block contains finally statements is a key tool for preventing resource leaks. At the time of closing a file or in the situation when recovering resources, place the code in a finally block to ensure that resource is always recovered.

The keyword throws indicates that a method can throw an exception that it does not handle. This declaration does permit another method to handle the exception. All Java methods use the throw statement for the purpose of throw an exception. The statement containing throw requires a single argument: a throwable object.

The mechanism of exception handling is composed of the following three operations:

*   The operation of declaring exceptions: According to specification, a method must specify the checked exception types in the method signature part, which the method may throw from its body, with the help of the keyword throws.
*   The operation of throwing an exception: At the time of encountering an abnormal scenario by a Java operation, the method having the statement that is erroneous, shall create a proper Exception object and throw the object to the Java runtime system with the help of the statement throw.
*   The operation of catching an exception: At the time of throwing an exception by a method, the JVM scans the call stack for an exception handler that is required. The specific exception handler can only handle a single type of exception. An exception handler that handles a specified class, can able to handle its subclasses also. In that case when no exception handler is identified in the call stack, the application exits.

Now, after discussing about definition and types of errors and exceptions, now we are going to present the concept of the try-catch-finally block of code in Java.

[Exception handling using try-catch-finally block](#contents.xhtml_sc2_215a)

There are some important points about try and catch block, listed as follows. Programmers must keep these properties into mind while they are creating applications.

*   The block try is likely to cause an error condition and throw an exception.
*   It can have one or more statements that could generate an exception.
*   Insert a catch block, just after try block.
*   No program code is allowed in between try and catch block in Java.
*   The statement after exception never be executed.
*   Nested try block may be in a program. If the inner try block does not have any catch statement, the control is passed to the outer try block where the catch block is present.
*   More than one catch block is possible. The catch block is called accordingly to the exception type present in the code.

We will choose to handle an exception for the two purposes; firstly, it allows us to resolve the error. Second, it stops the automatic termination of the program. Now, the actual source codes of try-catch-finally statements are given one after another in the next section for better understanding.

[Example of single try-catch block](#contents.xhtml_sc3_216a)

In the following [Figure 7.11](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-11), a simple coding example of try…catch block is demonstrated:

![](images/Figure_7.11.png)

Figure 7.11: Example of single try-catch block

Figure 7.12: Output of try-catch block

After the try…code block, include a catch clause that represents the exception type that you want to catch. You have to notice that the call to program code System.out.println("This will not be printed") inside the try code block is never executed.

[Example of single try-multiple catch block](#contents.xhtml_sc3_217a)

In the preceding [Figure 7.13](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-13), more than one exception has raised by a single piece of code like ArrayIndexOutOfBoudsException and ArithmeticException. In order to handle this type of scenarios, you can use two or more catch clauses, each for different exception types. At the time of an exception is thrown, each catch statement is examined in specific order, and the first one whose type is similar of the exception type is executed. If one catch statement executes, then the other catch statements are bypassed like catch(ArithmeticException e){System.out.println(e);}, and execution proceed with after the try-catch block.

[Example of throw keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc3_219a)

In the examples discussed so far, catching exceptions are presented in codes and thrown by the JVM. However, there may be some situations to throw an exception explicitly, with the help of the throw statement. The general syntax of throw statement is shown here:

throw ThrowableInstance;

where, ThrowableInstance which is an object of Throwable type or a child class of Throwable class. The data types like int or char), cannot be considered as exceptions. Also, the String and Object which are non-Throwable classes, cannot be considered as exceptions. In the [Figure 7.17](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-17), the source code with throw keyword is given:

![](images/Figure_7.17.png)

Figure 7.17: Example of throw keyword

The output of the preceding code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.18.png)

Figure 7.18: Output of throw keyword

[Example of throws keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc3_220a)

The throws clause is used to specify exception types which are thrown outside of a method. If a method causing an exception does not want to handle the exception, it must use the throw keyword for the caller method that calls the method. The throws clause declares the lists of exception types a method may throw. The throws keyword is used for checked Exceptions only. In the [Figure 7.19](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-19), the example is showcased:

![](images/Figure_7.19.png)

Figure 7.19: Example of throws keyword

The output of the preceding code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.20.png)

Figure 7.20: Output of throws keyword.

From the preceding example, it can be concluded that FileNotFoundException is thrown by the main() method. The main() method does not handle the exception. It is handled in runtime. So, we can conclude that the throws keyword can only throw checked exceptions.

[Difference between throw and throws keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc3_221a)

In the following [Table 7.3](#ch07.xhtml_tab7-3), the differences are given for the keywords throw and throws with syntax:

| Throw | Throws |
| --- | --- |
| The keyword throw is written inside a method for the purpose of throwing an Exception logically as and when required. | The keyword throws is written in the method signature. It is required at the time of a method contains some statements leading to some exceptions. |
| The keyword throw is utilized to throw an exception explicitly only one at a time. | The keyword throws can be used to declare a list of more than one exceptions, delimited by comma. According to the occurrence of the exception types, if the declaration matched, the exception is thrown automatically. |
| The throw keyword is used for unchecked exceptions. | The throws keyword is used for checked Exceptions only. |
| Syntax:class TstExp7 {static void cal(int i){if (i==5){Throw new ArithmeticException("Termination");}else{System.out.println("Its ok");}}public static void main(String args[]) {try{cal(5);}catch(ArithmeticException e){System.out.println(e);}}} | Syntax:void calculation() throws ArithmeticException, NullPointerException{// Statements where exceptions might occur.throw new ArithmeticException();} |

Table 7.3: Difference between throw and throws keyword in Java

[Finally keyword](#contents.xhtml_sc3_222a)

There will be a problem, if a method opens a file on enter and closes it on exit. You do not want the code that closes the file to be skipped by the exception handling mechanism. The finally keyword is intended to remedy this. The keyword finally produces a block of code that will be performed after a try-catch block and before the following code. Irrespective of the situation, whether an exception is thrown or not, the finally block will run. The code block of finally will be executed even if no catch statements match the exception. The example is given in the following [Figure 7.21](#ch07.xhtml_fig7-21):

![](images/Figure_7.21.png)

Figure 7.21: Example of Finally block

The output of the above code is as follows:

![](images/Figure_7.22.png)

Figure 7.22: Output of Finaly block

[Difference between final, finally and finalize](#contents.xhtml_sc3_223a)

The important differences between Final, Finally and Finalize are tabulated in the following [Table 7.4](#ch07.xhtml_tab7-4):

| Final | Finally | Finalize |
| --- | --- | --- |
| It is a keyword | It is a block of code | It is a method |
| It restricts the overriding of a member, method or a class. | Irrespective of the situation where, an exception is thrown or not, the finally block will run. The code block of finally will be executed even if no catch statements match the exception. | finalize( ) is only called just prior to garbage collection. |
| SyntaxFinal int a=10; | SyntaxFinally{…} | Syntaxfinalize(){..} |

Table 7.4: Difference between Final, Finally and Finalize keyword in Java

[Advantages of exception](#contents.xhtml_sc2_224a)

In the next section, the different advantages of exception are discussed in detail with pseudocode.

[Advantage of separating error-handling code](#contents.xhtml_sc3_225a)

Exceptions allow you to isolate the specifics of what to do when something unusual occurs from the primary logic of a program. The mechanisms like error detection, reporting, and handling in traditional programming frequently results in complex spaghetti code. Consider the following pseudocode, which reads a file into memory entirely:

Read_file {

opening the file;

determining its size;

allocating that much memory;

reading the file into memory;

closing the file;

}

The exceptions allow you to write your code with the main flow and to deal with the exceptional cases. If the Read_file method takes the help of the exceptions in lieu of traditional error management schemes, the code looks like the following:

Read_file {

try {

opening the file;

determining its size;

allocating that much memory;

reading the file into memory;

closing the file;

} catch (File_open_failed) {

Do_something;

} catch (Size_determination_failed) {

Do_something;

} catch (Memory_allocation_failed) {

Do_something;

} catch (Read_failed) {

Do_something;

} catch (File_close_failed) {

Do_something;

}

}

Make a note of the fact that exceptions do not spare the effort you give for the task of detecting, reporting, and handling errors, but they help to organize the work more effectively.

[Advantage of propagating errors up the all stack](#contents.xhtml_sc3_226a)

As the second advantage of exceptions, it is the ability to spread the error reporting up the call stack of methods. In the following example, the Read_file method is placed in the third position of nested method calls in a series made by the main program:

Methd1 {

call Methd2;

}

Methd2 {

call Methd3;

}

Methd3 {

Call_read_file;

}

Any exceptions thrown within a method can be avoided, allowing a method further up the call stack to catch them. As a result, only techniques concerned with mistakes must be concerned with error detection, shown as follows:

Methd1 {

try {

call Methd2;

} catch (exception e) {

Do_error_processing;

}

}

Methd2 throws exception {

call Methd3;

}

Methd3 throws exception {

Call_read_file;

}

However, as seen by the pseudocode, avoiding an exception necessitates some effort on the side of the intermediary techniques. Any checked exceptions that a method can throw must be defined in its throws clause.

[Grouping and differentiating error types](#contents.xhtml_sc2_227a)

Considering all exceptions thrown inside a program are objects, exception grouping or classifying is a logical consequence of the class hierarchy. IOException and its successors are an example of a series of related exception classes in the Java platform specified in java.io. The most generic exception is IOException, which describes any sort of error that might occur during I/O. Its offspring indicate more particular mistakes. A FileNotFoundException, for example, indicates that a file could not be found on disk.

A method can take the help of writing a particular exception handler to handle a very specific exception. According to the fact that the FileNotFoundException class has no descendants, in the specified , the specified handling mechanism can handle only one type of exception like catch (FileNotFoundException e) {...}. By providing any of the exceptionʼs superclasses in the catch statement, a method can catch an exception depending on its group or general type. An exception handler, for example catch (IOException e) {...}, specifies an IOException parameter to capture all I/O errors, independent of their exact type.

The ability of the handler is to capture all I/O errors, such as FileNotFoundException and EOFException. By querying the parameter supplied to the exception handler, you may learn more about what happened. To print the stack trace, you have to write e.printStackTrace().

At the end of the detailed discussion of errors and exception, one very specific scenario of exception handling is going to be discussed in the context of method overriding, that is very useful for real life application development.

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_229a)

In this chapter, the learners get detailed information about the errors and exceptions along with their types, classification, and handling operations. Important concepts like try-catch-finally block are also included as important topics of discussion. Last but not least, how the overridden methods can throw exception types. The examples of each concept are elaborated in a simple manner for better understanding.

In the next chapter, the concept of package and ways of package creation will be discussed. The features and advantages of packages are also explained, along with their applications..

# [CHAPTER 8Package Creation in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch08a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_234a)

Packages are an essential notion in Java. Java uses packages to eliminate name conflicts and manage access to classes, interfaces, and enumerations. The Java platform has an extensive class library (a collection of packages) that may be used in your own applications. This library is called the Application Programming Interface (API). Its packages cover the most typical tasks connected with programming for general use. A String object, for example, consists of state and behavior for character strings; a File object enables a programmer to easily create, delete, inspect, compare, or modify a file on the file system; a Socket object enables the creation and use of network sockets; and various GUI objects control buttons, check boxes, and other aspects of graphical user interfaces. There are literally hundreds of courses to pick from. This enables you, the programmer, to concentrate on the design of your specific application rather than the infrastructure needed to make it work.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_235a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Definition of package
*   Importance of packages
*   Features of packages
*   Types of packages
*   Key considerations of Java packages
*   Calculator program using a package

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_236a)

This chapter discusses the fundamental ideas of package design, rules for package creation, package features, types of packages (built-in packages and user-defined packages) used in Java programming, the compilation process with the -d option, and a calculator application that uses a package. In addition, the learners will obtain hands-on experience using the calculator program designed with the help of packages. Now, to start the discussion we have chosen the definition of package in the next paragraph.

[Definition of package](#contents.xhtml_sc2_237a)

A package is a collection of linked types that offers name space management and access control. Keep in mind that the term types include classes, interfaces, enumerations, and annotation types. In Java, similar classes are grouped together in packages. A folder in a file directory comes to mind. By using packages, we can reduce name conflicts and create more dependable code.

[Rules for creating packages](#contents.xhtml_sc3_238a)

The rules for creating packages are as follows:

*   To prevent conflicts with class or interface names, package names are composed in all lowercase.
*   Companies utilize their inverted Internet domain name to start their package names, such as com.abc.mypack for a package called mypac written by a programmer at abc.com.
*   Name clashes within one organization must be managed by internal convention, such as adding the region or project name after the company name (for example, com.abc.reg.mypack).
*   The packages within the Java program must begin with java or javax.
*   In some circumstances, the domain of the internet name may not be an acceptable package name. This can happen if the domain name has a hyphen or another special character. If the package name starts with a number or another character that is forbidden to use as the first character of a Java name, or if the package name contains a reserved Java keyword, like int. In this case, the advised norm is to use an underscore. The examples are given in the following table:

    | Name of the domain | Package name prefix |
    | abc-name.example.org | org.example.abc_name |
    | abc.int | int_.abc |
    | 123name.abc.com | com.abc._123name |

Table 8.1: Representation of the package name

Now, we are going to discuss why packages are useful for application development with their features.

[Importance of packages](#contents.xhtml_sc2_239a)

The benefits of using Packages in Java are as follows:

*   The packages organize the group of classes into a single API unit.
*   It will control the naming conflicts.
*   The access protection will be easier. Protected and default are the access level control to the package.
*   Easy to locate the related classes.
*   Reuse the existing classes in packages.

[Features of a package](#contents.xhtml_sc2_240a)

The following are the features of a package:

*   Every class is part of some package.
*   All classes in a file are part of the same package.
*   You can specify the package using a package declaration.
*   Package name as the first (non-comment) line in the file.
*   Multiple files can specify the same package name.
*   If no package is specified, the classes in the file go into a special unnamed package (the same unnamed package for all files).
*   If package name is specified, the file must be in a subdirectory called name (i.e., the directory name must match the package name).
*   You can access public classes in another (named) package using:

    An example is given as follows:

    package-name.class-name

    *   You can access the public fields and methods of such classes using:

        package-name.class-name.field-or-method-name

    *   You can avoid having to include the package-name using:

        import package-name.*;

        or

        import package-name.class-name;

    At the beginning of the file (after the package declaration). The former imports all of the classes in the package, and the second imports just the named class. You must still use, class-name to access the classes in the packages, and class-name.field or method-name to access the fields and methods of the class; the only thing you can leave off is the package name.

In the next section, the different types of packages are discussed with proper examples.

[Types of packages](#contents.xhtml_sc2_241a)

The packages are divided into two categories, listed as follows:

*   Built-in packages (packages from the Java API)
*   User-defined packages (create your own packages)

Let us proceed with the basic concepts of built-in packages provided by Java programming language.

[Built-in packages](#contents.xhtml_sc3_242a)

The built-in packages are from the Java API. The Java API is the library of pre-defined classes available in the Java Development Environment. The few built-in packages are as follows:

*   java.lang: Bundles the fundamental classes
*   java.io: Bundle of input and output function classes
*   java.awt: Bundle of abstract window toolkit classes
*   java.swing: Bundle of windows application GUI toolkit classes
*   java.net: Bundle of network infrastructure classes
*   java.util: Bundle of collection framework classes
*   java.applet: Bundle of creating applet classes
*   java.sql: Bundle of related data processing classes

[Importing built-in packages](#contents.xhtml_sc4_243a)

To import a built-in package, you should write the statement import java.util.Scanner.

Here, the components of the above statement are given:

*   Java is a top-level package
*   Util is a sub package
*   Scanner is a class which is present in the sub package util.

A simple program package is demonstrated as follows:

import java.util.Scanner; // Import the Scanner class

class tstPDPack {

public static void main(String[] args) {

Scanner mobj = new Scanner(System.in); // To create a Scanner object

System.out.println("Enter the name");

String urName = mobj.nextLine(); // Read user input

System.out.println("My name is: " + urName); // Output user input

}

}

The filename should be tstPDPack.java. The Scanner class, which is part of the java.util package, is used to capture user input. To utilize the Scanner class, make an object of the class and use any of the methods listed in the Scanner specification. In our example, we have utilized the nextLine() function, which reads strings. The output is given in the [Figure 8.1](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-1):

![](images/Figure_8.1.png)

Figure 8.1: Importing predefined packages in Java program

[User-defined packages](#contents.xhtml_sc3_244a)

User-defined packages are ones that the developer has built or generated to categorize classes interfaces avoiding naming conflicts. They are very similar to the built-ins that Java provides. It may be imported into other classes and used in the same way that we use built-in packages.

[Creating and importing user-defined packages](#contents.xhtml_sc4_245a)

To create package definition, you should first write package name, the class and the method definition in .java file. Suppose, pack1 is the name of the package we want to create. you insert the package pack1; in the .java file as tstpck.java. Then, you should write the body of your own class and save the file, as shown in the following:

package pack1;

public class tstpck

{

public static void disp()

{

System.out.println("Package created");

}

}

The output of the preceding program is as follows:

![](images/Figure_8.2.png)

Figure 8.2: Compilation of package definition with –d option

The steps for creating the package are as follows:

*   Save the file with .java extension (test folder)
*   Compile the file (.java) with the command javac –d . filename.java

If these two statements of the .java file tstpck.java are executed successfully, a folder named as pack1 will be created under bin directory and a .class file will be created under the folder pack1, named as tstpck.class that will hold the method definition. The [Figure 8.3](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-3) demonstrates the directory structure:

![](images/Figure_8.3.png)

Figure 8.3: Directory structure after compilation of package definition

The steps for importing the package are written as follows:

1.  Create a new file (pckTest.java) and import the package pack1.
2.  Import the package pack1 and the desired class that is tstpck.
3.  Create a new class as per your need that is pckTest.
4.  Save as pckTest.java in the folder test, as shown in the following figure:

    import pack1.tstpck;

    public class pckTest

    {

    public static void main(String s[])

    {

    tstpck p=new tstpck();

    p.disp();

    }

    }

5.  Compile the file (pckTest.java).

    The output of the preceding program is as follows:

    ![](images/Figure_8.4.png)

    Figure 8.4: Output after importing the user defined package

From the preceding [Figure 8.4](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-4), it can be concluded that the statement import pack1.tstpck; has executed properly and the output is produced Package created.

The following [Figure 8.5](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-5) demonstrates the directory structure, when importing the newly created package:

![](images/Figure_8.5.png)

Figure 8.5: Importing the newly created package

[Compilation process with –d option](#contents.xhtml_sc3_246a)

The argument -d tells the compiler to put the compiled code (.class files) into the class directory, within the right package structure.

![](images/Figure_8.6.png)

Figure 8.6: Importing the newly created package

The option d specifies the directory. Set the class fileʼs destination directory. The target directory must already exist; javac does not create it. If a class is part of a package, javac places the class file under a subfolder with the package name, generating directories as needed. For example, if you specify -d project/class/A and the class is called project.class.A.Tstpck, then the class file is called /project/class/A/Tstpck.class. From the preceding [Figure 8.6](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-6), the directory structure can be understood very clearly.

If -d is not specified, javac puts the class file in the same directory as the source file.

[Key considerations of Java packages](#contents.xhtml_sc2_247a)

The following are the key consideration of a Java package:

*   Classes not placed in a named package belong to the (anonymous) default package associated with the current directory, and when writing small programs. We generally just leave everything in the default package for simplicity. However, packages are there to help us keep our sanity when writing medium to large programs, and we should make use of them.
*   Members of classes in a given package that have the protected qualifier are available anywhere within the same package, and also from any subclass of a class in the given package that appears in another package.
*   Members of classes in a given package that have the default access (no specific access qualifier) are available only within the given package.
*   The statement import java.awt.*; does not mean that you also get what is in the part of the library that you get by using this statement: import java.awt.event.*;.

    Note: Packages do not really contain other packages, even though the hierarchical naming scheme may imply that they do. For example, using the import statement

This should be clear if you just think for a moment about how package names relate to the directory structure used to store package files, but it is easy to make wrong assumptions about the package relationships when the package names are closely related.

[Calculator program using a package](#contents.xhtml_sc2_248a)

In this section, we are trying to demonstrate how to make a simple calculator with the help of user defined packages in Java. To do that, we are creating five different .java files that contains the package definitions. The package cal is created for the definitions of the five classes namely add, sub, mul, div and fact. The source code is given as follows. We also have to compile these package definitions using javac command individually.

package cal;

public class add{

public static float operation(float a,float b)

{

return a+b;

}

}

To compile the preceding source code write javac add.java:

package cal;

public class sub{

public static float operation(float a,float b)

{

return a-b;

}

}

To compile the preceding source code, write javac sub.java, sown as follows:

package cal;

public class mul{

public static float operation(float a, float b)

{

return a*b;

}

}

To compile the preceding source code write javac mul.java:

package cal;

public class div{

public static float operation(float a, float b){

if(b!=0)

return a/b;

return -999;

}

}

To compile the preceding source code write javac div.java:

package cal;

public class fact

{

public static float operation(int a)

{

float mul=1;

for(int i=1;i<=a;i++)

mul*=i;

return mul;

}

}

To compile the preceding source code, write javac fact.java. The complete code is as follows:

import cal.*;

import java.util.*;

public class calculate{

public static void main(String args[]){

int ch=0;

Scanner in=new Scanner(System.in);

System.out.println("Enter,\n 1 for addition\n2 for subtraction\n3 for multiplication\n4 for division\n5 for Factorial");

ch=in.nextInt();

float a,b;

int c;

switch(ch){

case 1:

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand : ");

a=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand : ");

b=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("The Result is : "+add.operation(a,b));

break;

case 2:

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

a=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

b=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("The Result is : "+sub.operation(a,b));

break;

case 3:

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

a=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

b=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("The Result is : "+mul.operation(a,b));

break;

case 4:

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

a=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

b=in.nextFloat();

System.out.println("The Result is : "+div.operation(a,b));

break;

case 5:

System.out.println("Enter the value of operand ");

c=in.nextInt();

System.out.println("The Result is : "+fact.operation(c));

break;

default:

System.out.println("INVALID OPTION");

}

}

}

In the preceding program, we have used the package cal using the statement import cal.*. Then using a menu driven program we have introduced five options like 1, 2,3,4,5 for addition, subtraction, multiplication, division and factorial operations. When user select one particular option, the corresponding calculator function is called. The output is given in the following [Figure 8.7](#ch08.xhtml_fig8-7):

![](images/Figure_8.7.png)

Figure 8.7: Calculator program using user defined package

[Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_249a)

This chapter explores the basic principles of package structure, package formation guidelines, package characteristics, and various kinds of packages, like built-in packages and user-defined packages. In this context, how they implemented Java programming is documented step-by-step. The compilation process using the -d option is also presented with pictorial representation. A package-based calculator application is demonstrated to simplify the ideas and usage of packages.

In the next chapter, the detailed discussion about Stream and file handling will be provided. Different types of Stream class will be presented in this context. Then concept of file handling will be elaborated.

[Sample questions](#contents.xhtml_sc2_250a)

1.  Define the types of packages with examples (Bloomʼs Level-1)
2.  Compare and contrast between predefined and user defined packages (Bloomʼs Level-2)
3.  Sketch the hierarchy of directories of a user defined package (Bloomʼs Level-3)
4.  Illustrate the concept of package with examples. (Bloomʼs Level-4)
5.  Create one user defined package to implement different operations regarding stack and queue in Java programming (Bloomʼs Level-5)

[Multiple choice questions](#contents.xhtml_sc2_251a)

1.  Which of these keywords is used to define packages in Java?
    1.  Pkg
    2.  package
    3.  pkg
    4.  Package
2.  Which of the following is the correct way of importing an entire package pkg?
    1.  import pkg.
    2.  Import pkg.
    3.  import pkg.*;
    4.  None of these
3.  Which of the following package stores all the standard Java classes?
    1.  lang
    2.  java.io
    3.  util
    4.  java
4.  How many types of Java packages can be possible?
    1.  1
    2.  2
    3.  3
    4.  4
5.  java.awt package consists of _____.
    1.  you will get compilation error
    2.  Bundle of network infrastructure classes
    3.  Bundle of creating applet classes
    4.  Bundles the fundamental classes
6.  The statement package name; specifies
    1.  You can invoke user defined
    2.  You can remove the package using a package declaration
    3.  You can specify the package using a package declaration
    4.  None
7.  A package can be __________.
    1.  a Directory or Folder with Java Classes
    2.  a collection of files of type Java Class, Interfaces, or Abstract Class
    3.  contains Java Classes written for a specific purpose or problem
    4.  all of the above
8.  User-defined packages are ________
    1.  ones that the developer has built or generated to categorize classes and interfaces avoiding naming conflicts.
    2.  ones that the developer has to categorize variables avoiding naming conflicts
    3.  ones that the developer has built or generated to categorize threads avoiding naming conflicts
    4.  None
9.  Members of classes in a given package that have the protected qualifier are available anywhere within the same package
    1.  True
    2.  False
10.  Members of classes in a given package that have the default access (no specific access qualifier) are available only within the given package
    1.  True
    2.  False

[Answers](#contents.xhtml_sc3_252a)

1.  b
2.  c
3.  d
4.  b
5.  a
6.  c
7.  d
8.  a
9.  a
10.  a

# [CHAPTER 9Stream and File Handling in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch09a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_254a)

In Java, a stream is a series of data that is ordered. Java offers streams as a broad method of handling data I/O. To enable input and output operations in Java, the Java I/O system links a stream to a physical layer. In this chapter, we are going to present the detailed concept of stream and its types with pictorial representation. Then different classes and methods of different stream classes will be discussed. Also, the file handling in Java is presented in this chapter. The relationship between streams and files are elaborated in this context.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_255a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Stream in Java
*   Types of streams in Java
*   Process of reading data
*   File handling in Java
*   Creating a file
*   Reading data from a file
*   Writing data in a file
*   Copying data from one file to another
*   Deleting a file

[Objectives](#contents.xhtml_sc2_256a)

In this chapter, the Stream will be discussed in detail. With the aim to learn the basic concepts of Stream class and its classification, the various types of Stream class are discussed. It is the pre-requisite for the file processing in Java. The classes and methods of Stream class will be elaborated with examples. Then, they will be used for basic file processing like creating a file, reading content of a file, copying the content of the files and deleting a file.

[Stream in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_257a)

A stream object may be defined as follows:

*   It may be an input stream or an output stream,
*   It is may be of two types, one is processing stream or an ordinary stream,
*   It can be a character- related stream or a byte-related stream,
*   It might be linked to several different places or sources.

In the next section, the different types of stream classes are going to be present.

[Types of streams in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_258a)

An I/O Stream represents an input source or an output destination. A stream can represent many different kinds of sources and destinations, including disk files, devices, other programs, and memory arrays. Streams support many different kinds of data, including simple bytes, primitive data types, localized characters, and objects. Some streams simply pass on data; others manipulate and transform the data in useful ways. A program uses an input stream to read data from a source, one item at a time. A program uses an output stream to write data to a destination, one item at a time. This situation is given in the following [Figure 9.1](#ch09.xhtml_fig9-1):

![](images/Figure_9.1.png)

Figure 9.1: How I/O stream works in Java

Java stream class can be divided into two kinds of streams. As for example, byte streams and character streams. In the following [Figure 9.2](#ch09.xhtml_fig9-2), the classification of stream class is given:

![](images/9.2.png)

Figure 9.2: Types of Stream in Java

Each type has input streams and output streams. Next, we are going to learn the concepts of different stream categories with different examples.

[Byte-oriented streams](#contents.xhtml_sc3_259a)

The objective of byte streams is general-purpose input and output. Raw bytes or simple data types can be used for data.

[InputStream](#contents.xhtml_sc4_260a)

The basic class for all classes needed to read bytes from a file, memory, or terminal is called InputStream. Since input stream is an abstract class, we are unable to make an object of it; nevertheless, we may read bytes from the input stream using its subclasses.

![](images/9.3.png)

Figure 9.3: Classification of InputStream in Java

In the preceding [Figure 9.3](#ch09.xhtml_fig9-3), the different types of InputStream are depicted. Now, the following [Table 9.1](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-1), gives the method and its description of the available methods under InputStream class:

| Method | Description |
| available() | Provides an estimate of the amount of bytes that can be read (or skipped) from this input stream so that the subsequent call to a method for this input stream wonʼt stall. |
| close() | Releases the system resources connected to this input stream and closes it. |
| mark(int readlimit) | Indicates where you are in this input stream right now. |
| markSupported() | Checks if the mark and reset methods are supported by this input stream. |
| read() | reads every next byte from the input stream. |
| read(byte[] b) | A certain amount of bytes is read from the input stream and placed in the buffer array b. |
| read(byte[] b, int off, int len) | Reads up to len bytes of data from the input stream into an array of bytes. |
| reset() | This stream is repositioned to the point where this input streamʼs mark method was last invoked. |
| skip(long n) | n bytes of data are skipped over and discarded from this input stream. |

Table 9.1: Methods of InputStream class

[FileInputStream](#contents.xhtml_sc4_261a)

Bytes are read from a file in a file system via a FileInputStream. It is dependent upon the host environment which files are available. FileInputStream is designed to read raw byte streams, like those seen in picture files. Consider using FileReader to read character streams.

[OutputStream](#contents.xhtml_sc4_262a)

The basic class for all classes that are used to write bytes to a file, memory, or terminal is called OutputStream. Since outputStream is an abstract class, we are unable to make an instance of it; nevertheless, we may write bytes to the output stream using its subclasses.

![](images/Figure_9.4.png)

Figure 9.4: Classification of OutputStream in Java

In the preceding [Figure 9.4](#ch09.xhtml_fig9-4), the different types of OutputStream are depicted. Now, the following [Table 9.2](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-2), gives the method and its description of the available methods under OutputStream class:

| Method | Description |
| --- | --- |
| close() | Releases the system resources connected to this output stream and closes it. |
| flush() | Flushes this output stream and forces any buffered output bytes to be written out. |
| write(byte[] b) | b.length bytes are written to this output stream from the designated byte array. |
| write(byte[] b, int off, int len) | Starts at offset off, writes len bytes to this output stream from the given byte array. |
| write(int b) | The given byte is written to this output stream. |

Table 9.2: Methods of OutputStream class

The Java programs utilize byte streams for 8-bit byte input and output. From InputStream and OutputStream, all other byte stream classes are derived. There are many byte stream classes. To demonstrate how byte streams work, we will focus on the file I/O byte streams, FileInputStream and FileOutputStream. Other kinds of byte streams are used in almost the same way; they differ mainly in the way they are constructed. Bytes are read from a file in a file system via a FileInputStream. It is dependent upon the host environment which files are available. FileInputStream is designed to read raw byte streams, like those seen in picture files. Consider using FileReader to read character streams.

[FileOutputStream](#contents.xhtml_sc4_263a)

Writing data to a file or to a File Descriptor is done via a file output stream. A fileʼs availability or ability to be produced is dependent on the platform that underlies it. Particularly, some platforms only let one FileOutputStream (or equivalent file-writing object) to open a file for writing at a time. In such cases, if the relevant file is already open, the constructors in this class will fail. The FileOutputStream is designed for outputting raw byte streams, like those seen in picture data. Use FileWriter if you want to write characters one after the other. In the next section, the character-oriented streams are elaborated.

[Character-oriented streams](#contents.xhtml_sc3_264a)

This type of stream is designed to hold character data. Data is converted from the 16-bit Java char format used inside programs to the externally utilized UTF format. The platform based on Java uses Unicode standards to store character values. This internal format is automatically translated to and from the local character set using character stream I/O. The local character set in Western locations is often an 8-bit superset of ASCII. I/O with character streams is not more complex than I/O with byte streams for the majority of applications. Using stream classes for input and output results in automated translation to and from the local character set. Instead of requiring additional work from the programmer, a program that employs character streams instead of byte streams instantly adjusts to the local character set and is prepared for internationalization.

[Reader class](#contents.xhtml_sc4_265a)

Reader stream classes are used to read characters from files. In many ways, they are identical to InputStream classes.

Now, the following [Table 9.3](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-3), gives the classes and their description of the available methods under Reader class:

| Class | Description |
| BufferedReader | Takes text from a character-input stream while buffering characters to make reading lines, arrays, and characters more efficient. |
| CharArrayReader | A character buffer that may be utilized as a character-input stream is implemented by this class. |
| InputStreamReader | A bridge between byte streams and character streams is an input stream reader: Using a given charset, it reads bytes and decodes them into characters. |
| PipedReader | Piped character-input streams. |
| FilterReader | Abstract class for reading filtered character streams. |
| PushbackReader | A character-stream reader that allows characters to be pushed back into the stream. |
| StringReader | A character stream whose source is a string. |
| LineNumberReader | A buffered character-input stream that keeps track of line numbers. |
| FileReader | Convenience class for reading character files. |

Table 9.3: Classes of Reader class

The following [Table 9.4](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-4), gives the methods with their description of the available methods under Reader class:

| Method | Description |
| --- | --- |
| close() | Terminates the stream and releases all related system resources. |
| mark(int readAheadLimit) | Indicates if the mark() action is supported by this stream. |
| markSupported() | Indicates if the mark() action is supported by this stream.. |
| read() | Reads a single character. |
| read(char[] cbuf) | Reads characters into an array. |
| read(char[] cbuf, int off, int len) | Inserts characters into a specific area of an array. |
| read(CharBuffer target) | Attempts to read characters into the specified character buffer. |
| ready() | Indicates if the stream is ready for reading. |
| reset() | Resets the stream. |

Table 9.4: Methods of Reader class

[Writer class](#contents.xhtml_sc4_266a)

The following [Table 9.5](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-5), gives the classes with their description of the available methods under Writer class:

| Class | Description |
| BufferedWriter | Writes text to a character-output stream, buffering characters so as to provide for the efficient writing of single characters, arrays, and strings. |
| CharArrayWriter | A character buffer that may be used as a Writer is implemented by this class. |
| PipedWriter | Piped character-output streams. |
| StringWriter | A character stream that collects its output in a string buffer, which can then be used to construct a string. |
| FileWriter | Convenience class for writing character files. |
| FilterWriter | Abstract class for writing filtered character streams. |
| PrintWriter | Prints formatted representations of objects to a text-output stream. |

Table 9.5: Classes of Writer class

The Writer class is used to write characters into files. In many way, they are identical to OutputStream classes.

The following [Table 9.6](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-6), gives the methods with their description of the available methods under Writer class:

| Method | Description |
| --- | --- |
| append(char c) | It appends the desired character to this Writer. |
| append(CharSequence csq) | It appends the desired character sequence to this writer. |
| append(CharSequence csq, int start, int end) | It appends a subsequence of the desired character sequence to this Writer. |
| close() | Closes the stream, flushing it first. |
| flush() | Flushes the stream. |
| write(char[] cbuf) | Writes an array of characters. |
| write(char[] cbuf, int off, int len) | Inserts characters into a specific area of an array. |
| write(int c) | Writes a single character. |
| write(String str) | Writes a string. |
| write(String str, int off, int len) | Writes a portion of a string. |

Table 9.6: Methods of Writer class

Now, with the detailed study regarding the streams, we are going to discuss how they are used in performing different I/O operations.

[Differentiating between character and byte stream](#contents.xhtml_sc3_267a)

The differences between character streams and byte streams are given in the [Figure 9.5](#ch09.xhtml_fig9-5):

![](images/Figure_9.5.png)

Figure 9.5: Difference between character and byte stream

[Standard input, output and error as predefined streams](#contents.xhtml_sc2_268a)

The java.lang package is automatically imported by all Java programs, as you may know. A class named System, which contains many run-time environment components, is defined in this package. Among its contents are three preset stream variables: err, out, and in. The system has designated these fields as final, public, and static. This indicates that they do not require a special system object to be used and may be utilized by any other section of your software.

The standard output stream is denoted by System.out. By default, it is known as Console. Standard input, which is the keyboard by default, is referred to as system.in. System.err is an abbreviation for the standard error stream, which by default is the console.

[Process of reading data](#contents.xhtml_sc2_269a)

There are four classes used to read data from keyboard and they are listed as follows:

*   InputStreamReader
*   Console
*   Scanner
*   DataInputStream

Let us discuss each class and the utility of each class:

*   InputStreamReader class: Data from the keyboard may be read using the InputStreamReader class. It has the responsibility to connect to the keyboardʼs input stream and transforms the byte-oriented stream into a character-oriented stream, among other things.

    A bridge between byte streams and character streams is an input stream reader. Using a given charset, it reads bytes and decodes them into characters.

*   BufferedReader class: The readLine() function of the BufferedReader class may be used to read data line by line. This function reads text from a character-input stream while buffering characters to make reading characters, arrays, and lines more successful. As it buffers input, it enhances performance.
*   DataInputStream: It is utilized to machine-independently read primitive Java data types from an underlying input stream. A data input stream can read data that an application writes via a data output stream. Multithreaded access to DataInputStream is not always secure.
*   Scanner class: A basic text scanner that uses regular expressions to parse strings and primitive types. Using a delimiter pattern that by default detects whitespace, a scanner divides its input into tokens. Afterwards, the tokens might be transformed into diverse values by utilizing the different subsequent techniques.

    For example, the following code allows a user to read a number from system.in:

    Scanner sc = new Scanner(System.in);

    int k = sc.nextInt(); // Scans the next token of the input as an int.

The java.util package has a class called Scanner, that is used to get input of primitive kinds such as strings and int, double, and so on. It is the simplest method for reading input into a Java program. Typically, the predefined object System.in—which stands for the standard input stream—is given when creating an object of the Scanner class. To read strings, we use nextLine().

[Different BufferedReader class methods](#contents.xhtml_sc3_270a)

The different methods under BufferedReader class are tabulated with their description in the following [Table 9.7](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-7):

| Method | Description |
| close() | Terminates the stream and releases all related system resources. |
| lines() | It delivers a stream with lines read from this BufferedReader as its constituent parts. |
| mark(int readAheadLimit) | Marks the present position in the stream. |
| markSupported() | Tells whether this stream supports the mark() operation, which it does. |
| read() | Reads a single character. |
| read(char[] cbuf, int off, int len) | Reads characters into a portion of an array. |
| readLine() | Reads a line of text. |
| ready() | Indicates if the stream will work for reading. |
| reset() | Resets the stream to the most recent mark. |
| skip(long n) | Skips characters. |

Table 9.7: Methods of BufferedReader class

[Different scanner class methods](#contents.xhtml_sc3_271a)

The scanner class methods and their descriptions are depicted in the following [Table 9.8](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-8):

| Methods | Description |
| nextChar() | To read a char, we use next().charAt(0). next() function returns the next token/word in the input as a string and charAt(0) function returns the first character in that string. |
| nextInt() | It is used to read an integer value from the keyboard |
| nextFloat() | It is used to read a float value from the keyboard |
| nextLong() | It is used to read a long value from the keyboard |
| hasNext() | If there is another token in the input of this scanner, returns true. While waiting for input to scan, this procedure can get stuck. There is no input that the scanner advances past. |
| next() | It locates and gives back the following full token from this scanner. Matching delimiter pattern input comes before and after a full token. This function may pause while it waits for input to be scanned, even if a prior hasNext() call returned true. |

Table 9.8: Methods of scanner class
