[Difference between Scanner and BufferedReader class](#contents.xhtml_sc3_274a)

The following [Table 9.9](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-9) presents the important differences between Scanner and BufferedReader classes:

| Scanner | Buffered reader |
| Small memory | Large memory |
| It is not synchronized | BufferedReader is synchronous. Preferred for multi-threading environment. |
| The Scanner has a little buffer (1KB char buffer) | BufferedReader (8KB byte buffer) |
| Slower. Because scanner does parsing of input data | BufferedReader is a bit faster |

Table 9.9: Difference between Scanner and BufferedReader class

[File handling in Java](#contents.xhtml_sc2_275a)

File class is an essential component of every program is file management. There are several ways to create, read, update, and delete files in Java. A file is a designated location used to hold pertinent information. The Java.io packageʼs File class gives us the ability to operate with files, create an object of the File class and provide the filename or directory name in order to utilize it. The different methods and constructors available under File class are given in the following [Tables 9.10](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-10) and [9.11](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-11):

| Method | Description |
| canRead() | Determines if the file can be read or not. |
| canWrite() | Determines if the file may be written to or not. |
| createNewFile() | Generates a blank file |
| delete() | It deletes a file |
| exists() | Verifies the existence of the file |
| getName() | It provides the filename back. |
| getAbsolutePath() | It delivers the fileʼs absolute pathname. |
| length() | It provides the fileʼs size in bytes. |
| list() | Returns an array of the files in the directory |
| mkdir() | Creates a directory |

Table 9.10: Methods available in File class

The different constructors are explained with description in the following [Table 9.11](#ch09.xhtml_tab9-11):

| Constructor | Description |
| File(File parent, String child) | Creates a new File instance from a parent abstract pathname and a child pathname string. |
| File(String pathname) | Creates a new File instance by converting the given pathname string into an abstract pathname. |
| File(String parent, String child) | Creates a new File instance from a parent pathname string and a child pathname string. |
| File(URI uri) | Creates a new File instance by converting the given file: URI into an abstract pathname. |

Table 9.11: Constructors available in File class

# [CHAPTER 10Applet Program in Java](#contents.xhtml_ch10a)

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_286a)

Applet is a small program in Java. It is not a standalone program. To implement applet program, we need the browser support. It has its own lifecycle. There are several methods present inside applet class to implement applet lifecycle. In this chapter, we are elaborating the building blocks of the applet programming using Java. To give clear pictures of each concept, we have taken simple programs. The HTML programming is also taken into considerations to run applet programs. There are several programming examples written in this chapter to demonstrate the utility and usability of applet programming building real life applications.

[Structure](#contents.xhtml_sc2_287a)

In this chapter, we will cover the following topics:

*   Definition of Applet
*   Hierarchy of Applet class
*   Applet lifecycle
*   Syntax of Applet
*   Applet program for changing background color
*   Applet program for drawing different shapes
*   Applet program for implementing status bar
*   Event handling

[Objective](#contents.xhtml_sc2_288a)

The aim of the chapter is to provide the concept of Applet programming and its use in real life application building. The structure and syntax of creation of an Applet program will be studied in detailed explanation in this chapter. The most important topic Applet Life-Cycle will be explained with a diagram. The applet class methods will be elaborated for learners to create real life applications. Different types of applications will be discussed in terms of Java programs. The concept of Event handling will be the last topic to be discussed along with programs.

[Definition of Applet](#contents.xhtml_sc2_289a)

The purpose of an applet is to be embedded into another application rather than run independently. Any applet that will be included in a web page or used by the Java Applet Viewer must have the Applet class as its super class. Between applets and their environment, the Applet class offers a standard interface. A Java applet is a particular type of Java program that may be downloaded and executed from the internet by a browser that supports Java technology. An applet generally operates in the context of a browser and is embedded into a web page. A subclass of the java.applet.Applet class is required for an applet. The standard interface between the applet and the browser environment is provided by the Applet class. The package java.applet contains the Applet class. Several methods in the applet allow you to have fine-grained control over how your applet is executed. All applets fall under the category of applet. As a result, all applets need to import java.applet. Furthermore, applets must import java.awt. The acronym AWT stands for Abstract Window Toolkit. Since every applet runs in a window, java.awt package import is required to add functionality for that window. The Java run-time interpreter for consoles does not run applets. Instead, they are either run by an applet viewer or a web browser. An applet does not start running at main(). System.out.println() does not produce output to the window of your applet. Instead, other AWT methods are used to handle it, such drawString(), which produces a string according to a given X,Y location. Input is also handled differently than in an application. The Applet tag contains properties for the appletʼs width, height, codebase, archive, name, and other properties that affect where the applet appears in the browser. You can give the name of the applet class that has to be loaded from the server using the code property. The dimensions of the applet instance within the browser are specified by its width and height.

[Advantage of Applet](#contents.xhtml_sc3_290a)

There are many advantages of applet. They are as follows:

*   Less response time as it operates at the client side.
*   Browsers on a variety of operating systems, such as Linux, Windows, Mac OS, etc., may use it.
*   Typically, applets are able to establish a network connection back to their source host.
*   HTML pages may be presented with ease by applets that operate within a web browser.
*   On the same page, applets can call the public methods of other applets.
*   There are no limitations applied to applets loaded over a network; instead, they are unrestricted while loading them from a location located in the userʼs CLASSPATH on the local file system.
*   Applets do not have to cease working when you leave their page, even if most do.

[Drawbacks of Applet](#contents.xhtml_sc3_291a)

There are certain limitations of applet in Java and they are as follows:

*   To run the applet, the client browser must have a plugin installed.
*   Native methods cannot be defined or libraries loaded by an applet.
*   Normally, it is unable to read or write files on the host it is running on.
*   It is only able to establish connections with the host from where it originated.
*   It is unable to launch any software on the host it is running on.
*   It is unable to read some system attributes.
*   The appearance of windows brought up by an applet varies from windows brought up by a program.

[Hierarchy of Applet class](#contents.xhtml_sc2_292a)

A subclass of the Applet class is created for each applet in order to implement it. The Applet classʼs inheritance hierarchy is depicted in the following figure. [Figure 10.1](#ch10.xhtml_fig10-1) illustrates how the Applet class extends Panel. Panel is an extension of Container, a Component subtype.

![](images/Figure_10.1.png)

Figure 10.1: Representation of Applet class hierarchy

[Applet life cycle](#contents.xhtml_sc2_293a)

The applet classʼs init() method has a blank implementation, but any initialization may be done by overriding it with an applet subclass. The browser keeps track of whether the applet instance is active or not. The applet instance is in an active state when the webpage that contains it is presently shown. The applet is then set to active by the browser using the start() function. The browser would call the stop() method on the instance and place it in an inactive state if the applet was becoming inactive since it was not on an active page. Therefore, depending on what the user does, the browser may call the start and stop methods several times. At last, the browser displays the message Web page containing applet is being destroyed. Subsequently, the browser would use the destory() function on the applet instance, indicating to the applet that it is about to be destroyed. The applet life cycle is shown in [Figure 10.2](#ch10.xhtml_fig10-2):

![](images/Figure_10.2.png)

Figure 10.2: Representation of Applet life cycle

[Applet class methods](#contents.xhtml_sc3_294a)

In this section, we are going to present the basic methods of applet class and their descriptions, mentioned as follows:

*   init(): This applet has been informed that it has been loaded into the system by the browser or applet viewer. Prior to the start method being called for the first time, it is always called.
*   start(): Invoked to instruct this applet to begin running by the browser or applet viewer. It is invoked each time an applet is viewed on a Web page, following the init method. It is invoked automatically when an applet starts, following init().
*   stop(): Invoked to instruct this applet to cease running by the browser or applet viewer. It is called both shortly before the applet is about to be deleted and after the Web page containing it has been replaced with another one. An applet is restarted when the browser uses start() after it has been stopped.
*   destroy(): Triggered by the browser or applet viewer to notify this applet that any resources it has allocated should be destroyed as it is being reclaimed. Always invoke the stop method before destroying.
*   getAppletContext():  Provides the context for this applet, enabling it to interact with and learn from its running environment.
*   getAppletInfo(): Gives back details about this applet.
*   getAudioClip(URL): Gives back the AudioClip object that was sent in as the URL parameter.**
*   getAudioClip(URL, String): Returns the AudioClip object specified by the URL and name arguments.
*   getCodeBase(): Gets the base URL.
*   getDocumentBase(): Gets the document URL.
*   getImage(URL): Gives back an Image object that can then be painted on the screen.
*   getImage(URL, String): Gives back an Image object that can then be painted on the screen.
*   getLocale(): Gets the Locale for the applet, if it has been set.
*   getParameter(String): Gives back the value of the named parameter in the HTML tag.
*   getParameterInfo(): Gives back information about the parameters than are understood by this applet.
*   isActive(): Tests if this applet is active.
*   play(URL): Plays the audio clip at the specified absolute URL.
*   play(URL, String): Plays the audio clip given the URL and a specifier that is relative to it.
*   resize(Dimension): Asks to have this applet resized.
*   resize(int, int): Asks to have this applet resized.
*   setStub(AppletStub): The stub of this applet is set.
*   showStatus(String): Requests that the argument string be displayed in the status window.

[Syntax of Applet](#contents.xhtml_sc2_295a)

The java.awt and java.applet are two packages that must be imported by any Applet application. The classes of the Abstract Window Toolkit (AWT) are imported by java.awt.*. Through the AWT, applets communicate with the user. The java.applet.* imports the class Applet from the applet package. The statement import java.awt.*; tells the compiler to include all the classes contained in java.awt package. Each Applet program has to declare a paint() function. The AWT class defines this method, which the applet has to override. An applet calls the paint() function each time it wants to -redisplay its output. The coordinate system of Java starts with top left corner as (x,y)=(0,0). For all the methods of drawing we have to specify the x and y coordinate system. To draw a text using applet program, we have to use drawString() inside or paint() method with the syntax like gaphicsObj.drawString(String,x-coordinate,y-coordiante). In our program, Graphics g  indicates g is an object of Graphics class. The statement g.drawString("Hello java",100,100); is used to draw a text Hello java with the location of (100,100) of our x and y coordinate system. In the following [Figure 10.3](#ch10.xhtml_fig10-3), the coordinate system is presented:

![](images/Figure_10.3.png)

Figure 10.3: Representation of coordinates

Let us write one simple Java applet program and demonstrate the output of the code. In our program Hellojava is the class name with public access modifier to run the program directly. To specify that our class is an Applet, we use extends Applet that inherits the properties of Applet class.

The creation of a simple Applet program is give as follows:

import java.applet.*;

import java.awt.*;

public class Hellojava extends Applet{

public void paint(Graphics g){

g.drawString(“A simple Applet program", 10, 100);

}

}

The sample HTML code is given as follows:

<html>

<body>

<applet code= Hellojava.class width=400 height=400> </applet>

</body>

</html>

The output of the preceding applet program is given in the following [Figure 10.4](#ch10.xhtml_fig10-4):

![](images/Figure_10.4.png)

Figure 10.4: Simple Applet program for displaying message

[Applet program for changing background color](#contents.xhtml_sc2_296a)

The following code is for background color of the page:

import java.awt.*;

import java.applet.*;

public class TstApplet1 extends Applet{

String msg;

// set the foreground and background colors.

public void init() {

setBackground(Color.cyan);

setForeground(Color.red);

msg = "Inside init( ) --";

}

// Initialize the string to be displayed.

public void start() {

msg += " Inside start( )-- ";

}

// Display msg in applet window.

public void paint(Graphics g) {

msg += " Inside paint( ).";

g.drawString(msg, 10, 30);

}

}

<html>

<body>

<applet code="TstApplet1" width="400" height="400">

</applet>

</body>

</html>

The output of the preceding program is given as follows:

![](images/Figure_10.5.png)

Figure 10.5: Simple Applet program for setting the background color

[Applet program for drawing different shapes](#contents.xhtml_sc2_297a)

The following code is for drawing different shapes:

import java.applet.Applet;

import java.awt.*;

public class TstApplet2 extends Applet{

public void paint(Graphics g){

g.setColor(Color.green);

g.drawString("Welcome",50, 50);

g.drawLine(20,30,20,300);

g.drawRect(70,100,30,30);

g.fillRect(170,100,30,30);

g.drawOval(70,200,30,30);

g.setColor(Color.pink);

g.fillOval(170,200,30,30);

g.drawArc(90,150,30,30,30,270);

g.fillArc(270,150,30,30,0,180);

}

}

<html>

</applet>

</body>

[Event handling](#contents.xhtml_sc2_299a)

An event is when an objectʼs state changes. Consider clicking a button, dragging the mouse, and so on, as examples. Numerous event classes and Listener interfaces are available for event processing in the java.awt.event package.

[Steps to perform event handling](#contents.xhtml_sc3_300a)

The following steps are required to perform event handling:

1.  Implement the Listener interface and overrides its methods
2.  Register the component with the Listener

Several event classes and Listener interfaces are available for event processing in the java.awt.event package. They are tabulated in the following [Table 10.1](#ch10.xhtml_tab10-1):

| Event classes | Listener interfaces |
| --- | --- |
| ActionEvent | ActionListener |
| MouseEvent | MouseListener and MouseMotionListener |
| MouseWheelEvent | MouseWheelListener |
| KeyEvent | KeyListener |
| ItemEvent | ItemListener |
| TextEvent | TextListener |
| AdjustmentEvent | AdjustmentListener |
| WindowEvent | WindowListener |
| ComponentEvent | ComponentListener |
| ContainerEvent | ContainerListener |
| FocusEvent | FocusListener |

Table 10.1: Description of Java event classes and listener interfaces

Now, in the next section, we are presenting simple programs that can use the ActionListener interface to create events in Java programming.

[Sample program for event handling](#contents.xhtml_sc3_301a)

First, use the applet to extend the class and implement the appropriate listener. Make the Button and Text-Field components. The button component was registered with the appropriate event. For example, addActionListener()ʼs ActionEvent. Apply the abstract procedure at the end. [Conclusion](#contents.xhtml_sc2_302a)

The notion of Applet programming and its prerequisites were first covered in this chapter. The applet class hierarchy has been described. This context also includes an example of the applet lifetime. The coding and output syntax for applet programs is provided. This chapter presents the methods used in the applet class. This chapter also covers the handling of events.

In the next chapter the different types of Java applications are given in terms of programs. The Java program for Stack and Queue class. The programs for Tic-tac-toe using Java will be there.

The symmetric and asymmetric key Encryption and Decryption algorithms will be implemented using Java programs. Program for Message sending using socket program will be presented

[Multiple choice questions](#contents.xhtml_sc2_303a)

1.  When an appletʼs output is shown, which of these functions is called?
    1.  paint()
    2.  display()
    3.  getPainted()
    4.  none
2.  Which of these methods may be applied to an applet to output a string?
    1.  String()
    2.  drawString()
    3.  println()
    4.  display()
3.  Of these, which one is an important part of the Abstract Window Toolkit (AWT)?
    1.  println()
    2.  main()
    3.  run()
    4.  paint()
4.  Which security limitations are frequently seen in applets?
    1.  Applets are unable to declare native methods or load libraries.
    2.  Every system property cannot be read by an applet.
    3.  Only a.
    4.  Both (a) and (b)
5.  Which of the following is a disadvantage for Applet?
    1.  It is secure
    2.  To run the applet, the client browser must have a plugin installed.
    3.  Less speed of response since it operates at the client side
    4.  Browsers on a variety of operating systems, such as Windows, Linux, and Mac OS, may use it.
6.  Which method only gets called once while your applet is running?
    1.  paint()
    2.  destroy()
    3.  init()
    4.  stop()
7.  What is called both when the applet has to reload its browser page and just after the start() method?
    1.  destroy().
    2.  paint()
    3.  init()
    4.  None
8.  Which of these packages has every class and methods needed for Javaʼs event handling?
    1.  java.awt
    2.  java.applet
    3.  java.event
    4.  java.awt.event
9.  Which of the following methods is used to set up a listener for keyboard events?
    1.  addKeyListener()
    2.  KeyListener()
    3.  eventKeyboardListener()
    4.  none
10.  In the context of event handling, what is a listener?
    1.  A listener is a method that is notified when an event occurs
    2.  A listener is a constructor that is called when an event occurs
    3.  A listener is an object that is notified when an event occurs
    4.  A listener is a variable that is notified when an event occurs

[Answers](#contents.xhtml_sc3_304a)

1.  a
2.  b
3.  d
4.  d
5.  b
6.  c
7.  b
8.  d
9.  a
10.  c

[Introduction](#contents.xhtml_sc2_306a)

In this chapter, first we have provided the source code of two of the most important data structures Stack and Queue. Then, the Tic-tac-toe game is demonstrated using Java program. Later, the two important symmetric key and asymmetric key algorithm AES and RSA have been implemented using Java programming for encryption and decryption. At the end, the socket programming has been added in form of message passing between two programs, chat application and file sending between client and server.

The Socket programming examples for sending messages will be demonstrated in this chapter for building chat applications. Also, file sending programs using Socket will be presented in this context.

[AES encryption and decryption](#contents.xhtml_sc2_312a)

The class Cipher provides the functionality of a cryptographic cipher for encryption and decryption. It forms the core of the Java Cryptographic Extension (JCE) framework.

In order to create a Cipher object, the application calls the Cipherʼs getInstance method, and passes the name of the requested transformation to it. Optionally, the name of a provider may be specified.

A transformation is a string that describes the operation (or set of operations) to be performed on the given input, to produce some output. A transformation always includes the name of a cryptographic algorithm (for example, AES), and may be followed by a feedback mode and padding scheme.

A transformation is of the form:

*   algorithm/mode/padding
*   algorithm

(In the latter case, provider-specific default values for the mode and padding scheme are used). For example, the following is a valid transformation:

Cipher c = Cipher.getInstance("AES/CBC/PKCS5Padding");

Using modes such as CFB and OFB, block ciphers can encrypt data in units smaller than the cipherʼs actual block size. When requesting such a mode, you may optionally specify the number of bits to be processed at a time by appending this number to the mode name as shown in the AES/CFB8/NoPadding and AES/OFB32/PKCS5Padding transformations. If no such number is specified, a provider-specific default is used. Thus, block ciphers can be turned into byte-oriented stream ciphers by using an 8-bit mode such as CFB8 or OFB8.

Modes such as Authenticated Encryption with Associated Data (AEAD) provide authenticity assurances for both confidential data and Additional Associated Data (AAD) that is not encrypted. (Please see RFC 5116 for more information on AEAD and AEAD algorithms such as GCM/CCM.) Both confidential and AAD data can be used when calculating the authentication tag (similar to a Mac). This tag is appended to the ciphertext during encryption and is verified on decryption.

Class KeyGenerator provides the functionality of a secret (symmetric) key generator. Key generators are constructed using one of the getInstance class methods of this class. KeyGenerator objects are reusable, that is, after a key has been generated, the same KeyGenerator object can be re-used to generate further keys.

There are two ways to generate a key: in an algorithm-independent manner, and in an algorithm-specific manner. The only difference between the two is the initialization of the object:

*   Algorithm-Independent Initialization: All key generators share the concepts of a keysize and a source of randomness. There is an init method in this KeyGenerator class that takes these two universally shared types of arguments. There is also one that takes just a keysize argument and uses the SecureRandom implementation of the highest-priority installed provider as the source of randomness (or a system-provided source of randomness if none of the installed providers supply a SecureRandom implementation), and one that takes just a source of randomness.

    Since no other parameters are specified when you call the above algorithm-independent init methods, it is up to the provider what to do about the algorithm-specific parameters (if any) to be associated with each of the keys.

*   Algorithm-Specific Initialization: For situations where a set of algorithm-specific parameters already exists, there are two init methods that have an AlgorithmParameterSpec argument. One also has a SecureRandom argument, while the other uses the SecureRandom implementation of the highest-priority installed provider as the source of randomness (or a system-provided source of randomness if none of the installed providers supply a SecureRandom implementation).

    In case the client does not explicitly initialize the KeyGenerator (via a call to an init method), each provider must supply (and document) a default initialization.

Every implementation of the Java platform is required to support the following standard KeyGenerator algorithms with the keysizes in parentheses:

*   AES (128)
*   DES (56)
*   DESede (168)
*   HmacSHA1
*   HmacSHA256

A secret (symmetric) key. The purpose of this interface is to group (and provide type safety for) all secret key interfaces.

Provider implementations of this interface must overwrite the equals and hashCode methods inherited from Object, so that secret keys are compared based on their underlying key material and not based on reference. Implementations should override the default destroy and isDestroyed methods from the Destroyable interface to enable sensitive key information to be destroyed, cleared, or in the case where such information is immutable, unreferenced. Finally, since SecretKey is Serializable, implementations should also override ObjectOutputStream.writeObject(java.lang.Object) to prevent keys that have been destroyed from being serialized.

Keys that implement this interface return the string RAW as their encoding format (see getFormat) and return the raw key bytes as the result of a getEncoded method call. (The getFormat and getEncoded methods are inherited from the Key parent interface.)

The package javax.crypto.spec classes and interfaces for key specifications and algorithm parameter specifications.

A key specification is a transparent representation of the key material that constitutes a key. A key may be specified in an algorithm-specific way, or in an algorithm-independent encoding format (such as ASN.1). This package contains key specifications for Diffie-Hellman public and private keys, as well as key specifications for DES, Triple DES, and PBE secret keys.

An algorithm parameter specification is a transparent representation of the sets of parameters used with an algorithm. This package contains algorithm parameter specifications for parameters used with the Diffie-Hellman, DES, Triple DES, PBE, RC2 and RC5 algorithms.

*   PKCS #1: RSA Cryptography Specifications, Version 2.2 (RFC 8017)
*   PKCS #3: Diffie-Hellman Key-Agreement Standard, Version 1.4, November 1993.
*   PKCS #5: Password-Based Encryption Standard, Version 1.5, November 1993.
*   Federal Information Processing Standards Publication (FIPS PUB) 46-2: Data Encryption Standard (DES)

The GCMParameterSpec specifies the set of parameters required by a Cipher using the Galois/Counter Mode (GCM) mode.

Simple block cipher modes (such as CBC) generally require only an initialization vector (such as IvParameterSpec), but GCM needs these parameters:

*   IV: Initialization Vector (IV)
*   tLen: length (in bits) of authentication tag T

In addition to the parameters described here, other GCM inputs/output AAD, Keys, block ciphers, plain/ciphertext and authentication tags) are handled in the Cipher class.

Please see RFC 5116 for more information on the AEAD algorithm, and NIST Special Publication 800-38D, NIST Recommendation for Block Cipher Modes of Operation: Galois/Counter Mode (GCM) and GMAC.

The GCM specification states that tLen may only have the values {128, 120, 112, 104, 96}, or {64, 32} for certain applications. Other values can be specified for this class, but not all CSP implementations will support them.

This class Base64 consists exclusively of static methods for obtaining encoders and decoders for the Base64 encoding scheme. The implementation of this class supports the following types of Base64 as specified in RFC 4648 and RFC 2045.

*   Basic: Uses The Base64 Alphabet as specified in Table 1 of RFC 4648 and RFC 2045 for encoding and decoding operation. The encoder does not add any line feed (line separator) character. The decoder rejects data that contains characters outside the base64 alphabet.
*   URL and Filename safe: Uses the URL and Filename safe Base64 Alphabet as specified in Table 2 of RFC 4648 for encoding and decoding. The encoder does not add any line feed (line separator) character. The decoder rejects data that contains characters outside the base64 alphabet.
*   MIME: Uses The Base64 Alphabet as specified in Table 1 of RFC 2045 for encoding and decoding operation. The encoded output must be represented in lines of no more than 76 characters each and uses a carriage return \r followed immediately by a linefeed \n as the line separator. No line separator is added to the end of the encoded output. All line separators or other characters not found in the base64 alphabet table are ignored in decoding operation.

Unless otherwise noted, passing a null argument to a method of this class will cause a NullPointerException to be thrown.

[RSA encryption and decryption](#contents.xhtml_sc2_313a)

Immutable arbitrary-precision integers. All operations behave as if BigIntegers were represented in twoʼs-complement notation (like Javaʼs primitive integer types). BigInteger provides analogues to all of Javaʼs primitive integer operators, and all relevant methods from java.lang.Math. Additionally, BigInteger provides operations for modular arithmetic, GCD calculation, primality testing, prime generation, bit manipulation, and a few other miscellaneous operations.

Semantics of arithmetic operations mimic those of Javaʼs integer arithmetic operators, as defined in The Java Language Specification. For example, division by zero throws an ArithmeticException, and division of a negative by a positive yield a negative (or zero) remainder. All the details in the Spec concerning overflow are ignored, as BigIntegers are made as large as necessary to accommodate the results of an operation.

Semantics of shift operations extend those of Javaʼs shift operators to allow for negative shift distances. A right shift with a negative shift distance results in a left shift, and vice-versa. The unsigned right shift operator (>>>) is omitted, as this operation makes little sense in combination with the infinite word size abstraction provided by this class.

Semantics of bitwise logical operations exactly mimic those of Javaʼs bitwise integer operators. The binary operators (and, or, xor) implicitly perform sign extension on the shorter of the two operands prior to performing the operation.

Comparison operations perform signed integer comparisons, analogous to those performed by Javaʼs relational and equality operators.

Modular arithmetic operations are provided to compute residues, perform exponentiation, and compute multiplicative inverses. These methods always return a non-negative result, between 0 and (modulus - 1), inclusive.

Bit operations operate on a single bit of the twoʼs-complement representation of their operand. If necessary, the operand is sign- extended so that it contains the designated bit. None of the single-bit operations can produce a BigInteger with a different sign from the BigInteger being operated on, as they affect only a single bit, and the infinite word size abstraction provided by this class ensures that there are infinitely many virtual sign bits preceding each BigInteger.

For the sake of brevity and clarity, pseudo-code is used throughout the descriptions of BigInteger methods. The pseudo-code expression (i + j) is shorthand for a BigInteger whose value is that of the BigInteger i plus that of the BigInteger j. The pseudo-code expression (i == j) is shorthand for true if and only if the BigInteger i represents the same value as the BigInteger j. Other pseudo-code expressions are interpreted similarly.

This class SecureRandom provides a cryptographically strong random number generator (RNG).

A cryptographically strong random number minimally complies with the statistical random number generator tests specified in FIPS 140-2, Security Requirements for Cryptographic Modules, section 4.9.1. Additionally, SecureRandom must produce non-deterministic output. Therefore, any seed material passed to a SecureRandom object must be unpredictable, and all SecureRandom output sequences must be cryptographically strong, as described in RFC 1750: Randomness Recommendations for Security.

A caller obtains a SecureRandom instance via the no-argument constructor or one of the getInstance methods:

SecureRandom random = new SecureRandom();

Many SecureRandom implementations are in the form of a pseudo-random number generator (PRNG), which means they use a deterministic algorithm to produce a pseudo-random sequence from a true random seed. Other implementations may produce true random numbers, and yet others may use a combination of both techniques.

Typical callers of SecureRandom invoke the following methods to retrieve random bytes:

SecureRandom random = new SecureRandom();

byte bytes[] = new byte[20];

random.nextBytes(bytes);

Callers may also invoke the generateSeed method to generate a given number of seed bytes (to seed other random number generators, for example):

byte seed[] = random.generateSeed(20);

[Message sending using socket program](#contents.xhtml_sc2_314a)

The package java.net provides the classes for implementing networking applications. The java.net package can be roughly divided in two sections:

*   A Low Level API, which deals with the following abstractions:
    *   Addresses, which are networking identifiers, like IP addresses.
    *   Sockets, which are basic bidirectional data communication mechanisms.
    *   Interfaces, which describe network interfaces.
*   A High-Level API, which deals with the following abstractions:
    *   URIs, which represent Universal Resource Identifiers.
    *   URLs, which represent Universal Resource Locators.
    *   Connections, which represents connections to the resource pointed to by URLs.

[Sockets](#contents.xhtml_sc3_315a)

Sockets are means to establish a communication link between machines over the network. The java.net package provides 4 kinds of Sockets:

*   Socket is a TCP client API and will typically be used to connect to a remote host.
*   ServerSocket is a TCP server API and will typically accept connections from client sockets.
*   DatagramSocket is a UDP endpoint API and is used to send and receive datagram packets.
*   MulticastSocket is a subclass of DatagramSocket used when dealing with multicast groups.
*   Sending and receiving with TCP sockets is done through InputStreams and OutputStreams which can be obtained via the Socket.getInputStream() and Socket.getOutputStream() methods.

This class Socket implements client sockets (also called just sockets). A socket is an endpoint for communication between two machines.

The actual work of the socket is performed by an instance of the SocketImpl class. An application, by changing the socket factory that creates the socket implementation, can configure itself to create sockets appropriate to the local firewall.

This class ServerSocket implements server sockets. A server socket waits for requests to come in over the network. It performs some operation based on that request, and then possibly returns a result to the requester.

The actual work of the server socket is performed by an instance of the SocketImpl class. An application can change the socket factory that creates the socket implementation to configure itself to create sockets appropriate to the local firewall.
